A categorical account of composition methods in logic Thanks: Tomáš Jakl was funded by the EXPRO project 20-31529X awarded by the Czech Science Foundation and was also supported by the Institute of Mathematics, Czech Academy of Sciences (RVO 67985840), Dan Marsden was supported by EPSRC grant EP/T00696X/1: Resources and co-resources: a junction between categorical semantics and descriptive complexity, Nihil Shah was funded by UKRI under UK government’s Horizon Europe funding guarantee grant EP/X028259/1.
Tomáš Jakl ORCID:0000-0003-1930-4904, Dan Marsden ORCID:0000-0003-0579-0323, Nihil Shah ORCID:0000-0003-2844-0828

Abstract
. We present a categorical theory of the composition methods in finite model theory – a key technique enabling modular reasoning about complex structures by building them out of simpler components. The crucial results required by the composition methods are Feferman–Vaught–Mostowski (FVM) type theorems, which characterize how logical equivalence behaves under composition and transformation of models. Our results are developed by extending the recently introduced game comonad semantics for model comparison games. This level of abstraction allows us to give conditions yielding FVM type results in a uniform way. Our theorems are parametric in the classes of models, logics and operations involved. Furthermore, they naturally account for the existential and positive existential fragments, and extensions with counting quantifiers of these logics. We also reveal surprising connections between FVM type theorems, and classical concepts in the theory of monads. We illustrate our methods by recovering many classical theorems of practical interest, including a refinement of a previous result by Dawar, Severini, and Zapata concerning the 3-variable counting logic and cospectrality. To highlight the importance of our techniques being parametric in the logic of interest, we prove a family of FVM theorems for products of structures, uniformly in the logic in question, which cannot be done using specific game arguments. This is an extended version of the LiCS 2023 conference paper of the same name.

1.
Introduction

Composition methods
constitute a family of techniques in finite model theory for understanding the logical properties of complex structures
[
Lib04
]
. One works in a modular fashion, building a structure out of simpler components. The larger structure can then be understood in terms of the logical properties of its parts, and how they behave under the operations used in the construction.

The first result of this type was proved by Mostowski
[
Mos52
]
, who showed that the first-order theory of the product of two structures
A\times B
is determined by the first-order theories of
A
and
B
. Later, Feferman and Vaught famously proved a very general result for first-order logic, which included showing that the same holds true for infinite products and infinite disjoint unions of structures of the same type
[
FV59
]
. Since then, many more Feferman--Vaught--Mostowski (FVM) theorems
1
1

1

The terser Feferman–Vaught type theorem is more common in the literature.
have been shown for various operations, logics and types of structures. These theorems have important applications in the theory of algorithms
[
Mak04
]
, for example in the development of algorithmic meta-theorems such as Courcelle’s theorem
[
CE12
]
.

For our purposes, the typical form of an FVM theorem for a fixed
n
-ary operation
H
and logics
L_{1},\ldots,L_{n+1}
is as follows.
Given structures
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
and
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}
,

\displaystyle\mbox{For all $i$,}\quad A_{i}\equiv_{L_{i}}B_{i},

(1)

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad H(A_{1},\,\ldots,\,A_{n})\equiv_{L_{n+1}}H(B_{1},\,\ldots,\,B_{n}).

Here
\equiv_{L}
denotes equivalence with respect to the logic
L
.
Typically,
n
is either one or two. For example, writing
\equiv_{FO}
for equivalence in first-order logic, Mostowski’s result is given in the form of (
1
) as:

A_{1}\equiv_{FO}B_{1}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptA_{2}\equiv_{FO}B_{2}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{implies}\kern 5.0ptA_{1}\times A_{2}\equiv_{FO}B_{1}\times B_{2}

A key tool in finite model theory for establishing that two structures are logically equivalent is that of a model comparison game.
Examples of such games are the Ehrenfeucht–Fraïssé
[
Ehr60
,
Fra55
]
, pebbling
[
KV92
]
and bisimulation games
[
HM80
]
, establishing equivalence in fragments of first order and modal logic.
FVM theorems are typically proven using these games, building a winning strategy for the composite structure out of winning strategies for the components.

Despite their usefulness, working with model comparison games often requires intricate combinatorial arguments that have to be carefully adapted with even the slightest change of problem domain. To mitigate the difficulty with using game arguments directly, finite model theorists introduced higher-level methods such as locality arguments or 0–1 laws to establish model equivalences or inexpressibility.

The recently introduced
game comonad
framework
[
ADW17
,
AS21
]

provides a new such tool. This provides a unifying formalism for reasoning about model comparison games, using categorical methods.
The definition of a comonad enables a transfer of results from the formally dual theory of monads, commonly encountered in the categorical semantics of computation and universal algebra
[
Mog89
,
Mog91
,
Man76
]
.
Game comonads are designed to encapsulate a particular model comparison game, and are the key abstraction allowing us to give uniform results, whilst avoiding making arguments specific to a particular logic or its corresponding game.

The early work on game comonads focused on capturing many important logics
[
AM21
,
CD21
,
MS22
]
, laying the foundations for further developments. More recent results recover classical theorems from finite model theory, as well as completely new results
[
Pai20
,
DJR21
,
AM22
,
Reg22
]
. See
[
Abr22
]
for a recent survey and
[
AR21
,
AR23
]
for an axiomatic formulation.

Until now, there has been no account of the composition methods with the game comonad framework.
We close this gap by introducing a novel method for proving FVM theorems within the game comonad approach, giving results uniformly in the classes of structures, operations and logics involved.
Instantiating the abstract results for our example game comonads yields concrete FVM theorems with respect to four typical fragments of first-order logic:

(1)

the positive existential fragment
, i.e. the fragment of first-order logic of formulas not using universal quantification or negations,

(2)

the existential fragment
i.e. the fragment of first-order logic of formulas not using universal quantification, and in which negation can only be applied to atomic formulas.

(3)

counting logic
, i.e. the extension of first-order logic with counting quantifiers,

(4)

full
first-order logic.

As is standard in finite model theory
[
Lib04
,
EF95
]
, it is natural to grade these logics by a resource parameter, such as quantifier depth.
Correspondingly, the game comonadic approach to grading is to consider collections of comonads
\mathbb{C}_{k}
, indexed by a resource parameter
k
.
As a source of concrete examples we shall consider three particular game comonads,
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
,
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
and
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
.
The pair
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
and
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
capture first-order logic, with their resource parameters being quantifier depth and variable count respectively. The comonad
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
encapsulates modal logic up to modal depth
k
.

Conventionally, proving an FVM theorem involves cleverly combining several winning model-comparison game strategies to form a winning strategy on the composite structures.
In our game comonads approach, we only need to find a collection of morphisms of a specified form to obtain an FVM theorem for the positive existential logic.
This collection of morphisms is a formal witness to a combination of strategies.
Perhaps surprisingly, to witness equivalence in the counting logic, the same collection of morphisms only has to satisfy two additional axioms which coincide with the standard definition of Kleisli law
[
MM07
]
.

Although of practical interest, the FVM theorems in Sections
3
and
4
are relatively straightforward to prove. We present them in detail for pedagogical reasons to develop the ideas gradually.
The abstract FVM theorem for the full fragment is more technically challenging. This theorem shows that two additional assumptions suffice to extend an FVM theorem from counting logic to the full logic.
We can rephrase these assumptions categorically as showing the operation lifts to a parametric relative right adjoint over an appropriate category.
Surprising connections arise with classical results in monad theory, generalizing the notion of bilinear map and its classifying tensor product from ordinary linear algebra
[
Koc71a
,
JMS22b
]
.

Our three running example game comonads
{\mathbb{E}_{k}},{\mathbb{P}_{k}},{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
are introduced in Section
2
, along with some necessary background on comonads.
Sections
3
,
4
and
5
all follow the same pattern. We introduce the categorical structure abstracting logical equivalence with respect to the studied logical fragment, establish the corresponding categorical FVM theorem and illustrate this with concrete examples.
The examples in Section
4
include a new refinement of a result by Dawar, Severini, and Zapata
[
DSZ17
]
, showing that equivalence in 3-variable counting logic without equality and with restricted conjunctions implies cospectrality.
In Section
7
we observe that techniques from Section
5
also automatically entail FVM theorems for the existential fragment.
Section
8
develops FVM theorems for products of structures, uniformly in the logic of interest, greatly generalising Mostowski’s original result. Unlike our methods, which are parametric in the choice of game comonad, a typical game argument cannot be used to prove such a result, as the game is tied to a specific choice of logic.

In fact, the game comonads
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
and
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
naturally deal with first-order logic
without equality
. There is a standard and very flexible technique for incorporating equality, and other “enrichments” of logics
[
AS21
]
. This is introduced and related to FVM theorems in Section
9
, and illustrated with further examples, including the handling of weak-bisimulation.

Related Work

Our work can be viewed as comprising model theoretic meta-theorems about the compositionality of logics, parameterised by both the logics and operations of interest.
From this perspective, the original Feferman-Vaught theorem
[
FV59
,
FV67
]
is essentially a meta-theorem parameterised by a form of generalised product, which can be specialised to yield specific compositionality results specifically for first-order logic. A similar generalised product meta-theorem is used in the setting of modal or temporal logics in
[
Rab01
,
Rab07
]
. This work considers specific extensions of modal logic of varying expressive power, but does not go as far as being parameterised by the logics of interest, as in the current work. As well as positive results, this work also considers failure of compositionality, which is outside the scope of the present work.

Surveys of useful compositionality results, for first-order, monadic and guarded second order logic can be found in
[
Mak04
,
BCL08
]
.

Preservation of elementary equivalence is studied from a rather different categorical perspective to our own in
[
BR18
]
. The compatible interaction of composition and observable behaviour is captured by bialgebraic semantics
[
TP97
]
. Although bialgebraic semantics has a rather different intention to the current work, the use of a form of distributive law to witness compositionality bares some intriguing similarity to our results.

Corollary 6.4 in
[
FR23
]
is the only other example of an FVM theorem in the setting of game comonads. It is in fact proved concretely, by making use of the fact that open pathwise-embeddings are precisely strong epimorphisms in the category of the so called
pp-trees

[
FR23
, Definition 5.4]
.
Our work on FVM theorems for game comonads has been motivated by our comonadic account of the Courcelle theorem
[
JMS22a
]
, where FVM theorems are an essential ingredient.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Categories of Relational Structures

We assume familiarity with the basic definitions of category theory, including categories, functors and natural transformations, as can be found in any standard introduction such as
[
AT10
]
or
[
Awo10
]
.
Background on comonads, and any less standard material is introduced as needed.

A
relational signature

\sigma
is a set of
relation symbols
, each with an associated strictly positive natural number
arity
.
A
\sigma
-structure
A
consists of a
universe
set, also denoted
A
, and for each relation symbol
R\in\sigma
of arity
n
, an
n
-ary relation
R^{A}
on
A
.
A
morphism of
\sigma
-structures

f\colon A\rightarrow B
is a function of type
A\rightarrow B
, preserving relations, in the sense that for
n
-ary
R\in\sigma

R^{A}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\quad\mbox{implies}\quad R^{B}(f(a_{1}),\ldots,f(a_{n})).

For a fixed
\sigma
,
\sigma
-structures and their morphisms form our main category of interest
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
. We will also have need of the category of pointed relational structures
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
. Here the objects
(A,a)
are a
\sigma
-structure
A
with a distinguished element
a\in A
. The morphisms are
\sigma
-structure morphisms that also preserve the distinguished element.

2.2.
Comonads

A
comonad in Kleisli form
on a category
\mathcal{C}
is a triple
(\mathbb{C},\varepsilon,(-)^{*})
where

•

\mathbb{C}
is an object map
\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C})\to\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C})
,

•

the
counit

\varepsilon
is an
\mathrm{obj}({\mathcal{C}})
-indexed family of morphisms
\varepsilon_{A}\colon\mathbb{C}(A)\to A
, for
A\in\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C})
, and

•

the operation
(-)^{*}
maps morphisms
f:\mathbb{C}(A)\rightarrow B
to their
coextension

f^{*}:\mathbb{C}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(B)
,

subject to the following equations:

\displaystyle(\varepsilon_{A})^{*}=\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}(A)},\quad\varepsilon_{B}\circ f^{*}=f,\quad(g\circ f^{*})^{*}=g^{*}\circ f^{*},

(2)

It is then standard
[
Man76
]
that
\mathbb{C}
can be equivalently presented in what is referred to as
comonoid form

(\mathbb{C},\varepsilon,\delta)
where
\mathbb{C}
is an endofunctor on
\mathcal{C}
and the rest are natural transformation: a
counit

\varepsilon:\mathbb{C}\Rightarrow{I\mkern-1.0mud}
(where
{I\mkern-1.0mud}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
is the identity functor) and a
comultiplication

\delta:\mathbb{C}\Rightarrow\mathbb{C}^{2}
. This triple is required to satisfy
\varepsilon\circ\delta=\mathrm{id}=\mathbb{C}(\varepsilon)\circ\delta
and
\delta\circ\delta=\mathbb{C}(\delta)\circ\delta
. These equations specify that
(\mathbb{C},\varepsilon,\delta)
form a comonoid at an appropriate level of abstraction.

Lemma 1
.

Every comonad in Kleisli form
(\mathbb{C},\varepsilon,(-)^{*})
induces a comonad in comonoid form
(\mathbb{C},\varepsilon,\delta)
where
\mathbb{C}
is extended to morphisms by setting
\mathbb{C}(f\colon A\to B)=(f\circ\varepsilon_{A})^{*}
and
\delta_{A}
is the coextension of the identity morphism
\mathrm{id}_{\mathbb{C}(A)}^{*}
.

Conversely, a comonad in comonoid form
(\mathbb{C},\varepsilon,\delta)
gives a comonad in Kleisli form
(\mathbb{C},\varepsilon,(-)^{*})
by setting
f^{*}=\mathbb{C}(f)\circ\delta_{A}
.
These translations are inverse to each other.

We now introduce the game comonads that we shall regularly refer to in our examples. Each is parameterized by a positive integer
k
. The first two comonads are defined over
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
.

Ehrenfeucht–Fraïssé comonad
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
:

The universe of
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)
consists of non-empty words

[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]

with
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}
from
A
and
n\leq k
.
The counit
\varepsilon_{A}\colon{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)\to A
maps
[a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}]
to
a_{n}
.

For an
m
-ary relational symbol
R\in\sigma
and
s_{1},\dots,s_{m}\in{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)
, set
R^{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{m})
iff:

(1):

the
s_{i}
are
pairwise comparable in the prefix order on words
, i.e. for every
i,j
either
s_{i}
is a prefix of
s_{j}
or vice versa, and

(2):

R^{A}(\varepsilon_{A}(s_{1}),\ldots,\varepsilon_{A}(s_{m}))
.

The coextension of
h:{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)\rightarrow B
is

h^{*}([a_{1},\,\ldots,\,a_{n}])\,=\,[h([a_{1}]),\,h([a_{1},a_{2}]),\,\ldots,\,h([a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}])].

Pebbling comonad
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
:

The universe of
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A)
consists of non-empty words

[(p_{1},a_{1}),\,\dots,\,(p_{n},a_{n})]

of unrestricted (finite) length such that
a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\in A
and
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}
are
pebble indexes
, that is,
p_{1},\dots,p_{n}\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
.
The counit
\varepsilon_{A}
maps
[(p_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(p_{n},a_{n})]
to
a_{n}
.

For
m
-ary
R\in\sigma
,
set
R^{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A)}(s_{1},\ldots,s_{m})
iff:

(1):

The
s_{i}
are pairwise comparable in the prefix order on words.

(2):

For
s_{i},s_{j}
with
s_{i}
a prefix word of
s_{j}
, the pebble index of the last pair of
s_{i}
does not reappear in the remaining elements of
s_{j}
.

(3):

R^{A}(\varepsilon_{A}(s_{1}),\ldots,\varepsilon_{A}(s_{m}))
.

For
h\colon{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A)\rightarrow B
, the coextension
h^{*}
acts on words as:

h^{*}([(p_{1},a_{1}),\ldots,(p_{n},a_{n})])\,=\,[(p_{1},h(s_{1})),\ldots,(p_{n},h(s_{n}))]

where
s_{i}=[(p_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(p_{i},a_{i})]
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

We say that a signature
\sigma
is a
modal signature
if it only has relational symbols of arity one or two.

Modal comonad
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
:

Defined over
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
for a modal signature
\sigma
, the universe of
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A,a_{0})
consists of paths in
A
starting from
a_{0}
, i.e. sequences

a_{0}\xrightarrow{R_{1}}a_{1}\xrightarrow{R_{2}}a_{2}\xrightarrow{R_{3}}\ldots\xrightarrow{R_{n}}a_{n}

such that
n\leq k
and, for every
i
,
R_{i}\in\sigma
and
R_{i}^{A}(a_{i-1},a_{i})
. The counit and coextension act as for
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
.

For a binary
R\in\sigma
, the relation
R^{{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A,a_{0})}
consists of the pairs
(s^{\prime},s)
where
s
is the extension of
s^{\prime}
by
a_{n-1}\xrightarrow{R}a_{n}
in
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A,a_{0})
. For a unary
P\in\sigma
,
P^{{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A,a_{0})}(s)
iff
P^{A}(a_{n})
.

{thmC}

[
[
ADW17
,
AS21
]
]

{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
,
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
and
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
are comonads in Kleisli form.

The intuition for each of these comonads is that they encode the moves within one structure during the corresponding model comparison game. We shall introduce their mathematical properties as needed in later sections. See
[
AS21
]
for detailed discussions of all three comonads.

3.
FVM theorems for positive existential fragments

For a comonad
\mathbb{C}
, we introduce the following two relations on structures:

•

A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}B
if there exists a morphism
\mathbb{C}(A)\to B
.

•

A\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}B
if
A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}B
and
B\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}A
.

It is a standard fact, formulated categorically in
[
AS21
]
, that

\displaystyle A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B\quad\mbox{if and only if}\quad

for every positive existential sentence
\varphi
of quantifier

\displaystyle\text{depth at most $k$},\ A\models\varphi\text{ implies }B\models\varphi.

Therefore
A\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
if and only if structures
A
and
B
agree on the quantifier depth
k
fragment. Similarly,
A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B
and
A\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B
correspond to the same relationships, but for the
k
variable fragment
[
ADW17
]
.

Remark 2
.

For conciseness, in this section, and Sections
4
,
5
and
8
, references to first-order logic implicitly means the variant
without equality
and with infinite conjunctions and disjunctions, as is common in finite model theory. We shall return to the variant with equality in Section
9
.
Note, however, that typically logical equivalence of two
finite
structures in a fragment of logic with infinite conjunctions and disjunctions is the same as in the corresponding fragment of first-order logic with finite conjunctions and disjunctions.

We now consider FVM theorems for
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
and
\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
, parametric in
\mathbb{C}
. To motivate our abstract machinery, we first consider a warm-up example, that is nevertheless sufficient to highlight the key ideas.
For a signature
\sigma
, and
\tau\subseteq\sigma
, the
\tau
-reduct
of a
\sigma
-structure
A
is the
\tau
-structure on the same universe which interprets all the relation symbols in
\tau
as in
A
.
We aim to show that equivalence in positive existential first-order logic with quantifier rank
{\leq}\,k
is preserved by taking
\tau
-reducts of
\sigma
-structures for any
\tau\subseteq\sigma
. We can view the
\tau
-reduct operation as a functor

(-)|_{\tau}:\mathcal{R}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}(\tau)

which sends a
\sigma
-structure to its
\tau
-reduct and a homomorphism of
\sigma
-structures
f\colon A\to B
is sent to the homomorphism
f|_{\tau}\colon A|_{\tau}\to B|_{\tau}
with the same underlying function as
f
has.

Observe that taking reducts admits an FVM theorem for the Ehrenfeucht–Fraïssé comonad
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
, viewed as a comonad on both
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{R}(\tau)
categories. Given the logical reading of
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
, it is an easy observation that
A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
implies
A|_{\tau}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B|_{\tau}
. Indeed, if the positive existential sentences of quantifier depth
{\leq}\,k
in signature
\sigma
that are true in
A
are also true in
B
, then the same must hold for the positive existential sentences in the reduced signature
\tau
.

In this case, the FVM theorem can be proved using an ad-hoc argument about the operation involved. However, to identify a general strategy, we would like to prove this fact categorically. Given a morphism
f\colon{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)\to B
in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
witnessing
A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
, we wish to construct an
\overline{f}\colon{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A|_{\tau})\to B|_{\tau}
witnessing
A|_{\tau}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B|_{\tau}
. To this end, observe that there is a morphism of
\tau
-structures

\kappa_{A}\colon{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A|_{\tau})\to{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)|_{\tau}

which sends a word
[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]
in
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A|_{\tau})
to the same word in
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)|_{\tau}
. Therefore,
\overline{f}
can be computed as the composite

{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A|_{\tau})\xrightarrow{\kappa_{A}}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A)|_{\tau}\xrightarrow{f|_{\tau}}B|_{\tau}.

(3)

It is immediate that, since the definition of
\kappa_{A}
was not specific to
A
, there is such a morphism for every structure. Consequently, we obtain the following trivial pair of FVM theorems:

\displaystyle A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\mbox{implies}\kern 5.0ptA|_{\tau}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B|_{\tau}

\displaystyle A\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\mbox{implies}\kern 5.0ptA|_{\tau}\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B|_{\tau}

As this exercise demonstrates, all that is required to prove an FVM theorem for the relations
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
or
\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
, and a unary operation
H
, is to define for every structure
A
a morphism of
\sigma
-structures
\kappa_{A}\colon{\mathbb{E}_{k}}(H(A))\rightarrow H({\mathbb{E}_{k}}(A))
. It is not difficult to see that the same proof goes through when we parameterise over the comonads involved and allow operations of arbitrary arity. We obtain our first abstract FVM theorem.

Theorem 3
.

Let
\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n}
and
\mathbb{D}
be comonads on categories
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{n}
, and
\mathcal{D}
respectively, and

H\colon\mathcal{C}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathcal{C}_{n}\to\mathcal{D}

a functor.
If for every
A_{1}\in\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C}_{1}),\,\dots,\,A_{n}\in\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C}_{n})
there exists a morphism

\mathbb{D}(H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n}))\xrightarrow{\!\!\!\overset{\kappa_{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}}}{\hskip 8.19447pt}\!\!\!}H(\mathbb{C}_{1}(A_{1}),\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n}(A_{n}))

(4)

in
\mathcal{D}
, then

A_{1}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}_{1}}B_{1},\;\ldots,\;A_{n}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}_{n}}B_{n}

implies

H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{D}}H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n})

The same result holds when replacing
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
with
\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
.

Proof 3.1
.

Let
f_{i}\colon\mathbb{C}_{i}(A_{i})\to B_{i}
be the morphism witnessing the relation
A_{i}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}_{i}}B_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Since
H
is a functor, the morphism
\overline{f}
defined as the composition

\mathbb{D}(H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n}))\xrightarrow{\ H(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\ \circ\ \kappa_{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}}\ }H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n})

witnesses the relation
H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{D}}H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n})
.

The corresponding statement for
\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
is obtained by applying the same argument also to the collection of morphisms
g_{i}\colon\mathbb{C}_{i}(B_{i})\to A_{i}
that witness
B_{i}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}_{i}}A_{i}
, for
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

Remark 4

(Infinitary Operations)
.

For readability reasons, we state this as well as the other abstract FVM theorems, such as Theorems
9
and
33
below, for operations of finite arities only. Nevertheless, all these statements hold verbatim for infinitary operations as well.

3.1.
Examples

{exa}

As our first simple application of Theorem
3
, we obtain an FVM theorem showing that
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
is preserved by taking the disjoint union
A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
of two
\sigma
-structures
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
.
The universe of
A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
can be encoded as pairs
(i,a)
where
i\in\{1,2\}
and
a\in A_{i}
.
For
r
-ary relation
R\in\sigma
, the interpretation
R^{A_{1}\uplus A_{2}}
is defined as

R^{A_{1}\uplus A_{2}}((i_{1},a_{1}),\dots,(i_{r},a_{r}))

if and only if there exists an
i
such that for all
1\leq j\leq r
,
i_{j}=i
and

R^{A_{i}}(a_{1},\dots,a_{r})
.
We define a morphism of
\sigma
-structures

\kappa_{A_{1},A_{2}}\colon{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{1}\uplus A_{2})\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{k}}A_{1}\uplus{\mathbb{P}_{k}}A_{2}

which sends a word
s=[(p_{1},(i_{1},a_{1})),\dots,(p_{n},(i_{n},a_{n}))]
in
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{1}\uplus A_{2})
to the pair
(i_{n},\nu(s))
,
where the word
\nu(s)
is the restriction of
s
to the pairs
(p_{j},a_{j})
such that
i_{j}=i_{n}
.
Since the definition of
\kappa_{A_{1},A_{2}}
is not specific to the structures
A_{1},A_{2}
, by Theorem
3
, the following holds:

\displaystyle A_{1}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{1}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptA_{2}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{2}

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad A_{1}\uplus A_{2}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{1}\uplus B_{2}

Consequently, the same statement holds with
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
replaced by
\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
.
The statement demonstrates that if both
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
, and
A_{2}
and
B_{2}
, satisfy the same sentences of the positive existential fragment of
k
-variable logic,
then the disjoint unions
A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
and
B_{1}\uplus B_{2}
also satisfy the same sentences of that fragment.
Similar
\kappa
morphisms can be defined to demonstrate that
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
,
\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
and also
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
and
\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
are preserved by taking disjoint unions of
arbitrary
sets of
\sigma
-structures.

Even in the case of relatively simple operations on structures, it may be the case that logical equivalence beyond this fragment is not preserved.

{exa}
[Counterexample]

For the modal fragment, a positive modal formula is one without negation or the
\Box
modality.
As shown in
[
AS21
]
, for pointed structures

A=(A,a)\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptB=(B,b)

in a modal signature,
A\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}B
if and only if for every positive modal formula
\varphi
of modal depth at most
k
,

A\models\varphi
implies
B\models\varphi
,
and therefore
A\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}B
if and only if the two structures agree on positive modal formulae with modal depth bounded by
k
.

Categorically, the disjoint union of
\sigma
-structures is their
coproduct
in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
.
The formal definition of coproducts is unimportant for this example, but we note that
coproduct of two
pointed

\sigma
-structures
(A_{1},a_{1})+(A_{2},a_{2})
in
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
is a quotient of their disjoint union, where the distinguished points
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are identified.
Unlike with
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
and
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
, the relations
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}
and
\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}
are
not
preserved by this operation on pointed
\sigma
-structures, essentially because the two components can interact.
This shows that, although coproduct FVM theorems are relatively simple, it is by no means automatic that they will hold.

{exa}

For every binary relation
R
in a modal signature
\sigma
, there is an operation
(A_{1},a_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\vee}}(A_{2},a_{2})
which combines the two pointed structures by adding a new initial point
\star
with
R
-transitions to
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
. Operations such as this are commonly found when modelling concurrent systems with process calculi
[
Sti01
,
Ros10
]
. Intuitively this operation avoids the interactions that caused problems in Counterexample
3.1
.

There is a morphism of pointed
\sigma
-structures

\kappa_{(A_{1},a_{1}),(A_{2},a_{2})}\colon\mathbb{M}_{k+1}((A_{1},a_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\vee}}(A_{2},a_{2}))\rightarrow{\mathbb{M}_{k}}((A_{1},a_{1}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\vee}}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}((A_{2},a_{2}))

All sequences in the domain are of the form

*\xrightarrow{R}(i,c_{0})\xrightarrow{R_{1}}\ldots\xrightarrow{R_{n}}(i,c_{n}),

with
i\in\{1,2\}
and each
(i,c_{j})\in A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
in the same component.
Then,
\kappa_{(A_{1},a_{1}),(A_{2},a_{2})}
must preserve the distinguished element, and for sequences of length greater than one it sends
*\xrightarrow{R}(i,c_{0})\xrightarrow{R_{1}}\ldots\xrightarrow{R_{n}}(i,c_{n})
to
(i,c_{0}\xrightarrow{R_{1}}\ldots\xrightarrow{R_{n}}c_{n})
.
Applying Theorem
3
yields the FVM theorem:

\displaystyle(A_{1},a_{1})\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}(B_{1},b_{1})\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0pt(A_{2},a_{2})\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}(B_{2},b_{2})

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad(A_{1},a_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\vee}}(A_{2},a_{2})\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{M}_{k+1}}(B_{1},b_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\vee}}(B_{2},b_{2})

Notice that the resource index actually
increases
from
k
to
k+1
because of the shape of
\kappa
.

{exa}

A nice feature of the generality of Theorem
3
is that for different comonads
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{D}
over the same category
\mathcal{C}
, a natural transformation between the functors
\mathbb{C}\Rightarrow\mathbb{D}
or, more generally, a collection of morphisms

\{\kappa_{A}\colon\mathbb{D}A\rightarrow\mathbb{C}A\mid A\in\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C})\}

can be seen as instances of (
4
), with
H
equal to the identity functor
{I\mkern-1.0mud}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
.
This allows us to provide semantic translations for the logics captured by game comonads, a question that had not previously been addressed.
For example, if we consider
\mathbb{P}_{2}
as a comonad over
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
where
\mathbb{P}_{2}(A,a_{0})
has distinguished point
[(0,a_{0})]
, then for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
and object
(A,a_{0})
we define a morphism of pointed
\sigma
-structures

\kappa_{(A,a_{0})}\colon{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A,a_{0})\rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{2}(A,a_{0})

which sends the sequence of transitions

a_{0}\xrightarrow{R_{1}}\ldots\xrightarrow{R_{n-1}}a_{n-1}\xrightarrow{R_{n}}a_{n}

in
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A,a_{0})

to the word in
\mathbb{P}_{2}(A,a_{0})
where elements are labeled by the parity of their position

[(0,a_{0}),(1,a_{1}),\dots,(n\bmod 2,a_{n})].

Applying Theorem
3
yields the well-known fact that structures which satisfy the same sentences of the positive existential fragment of two-variable logic must also satisfy the same positive modal formulas for any modal depth
k

\displaystyle(A,a_{0})\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{P}_{2}}(B,b_{0})

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad(\forall k\in\mathbb{N}.\ \ (A,a_{0})\equiv_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}(B,b_{0})).

Similarly, there exist morphisms

\kappa_{A}\colon{\mathbb{E}_{k}}A\rightarrow{\mathbb{P}_{k}}A
for every
A
in
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
where
[a_{1},\dots,a_{n}]
with
n\leq k
is sent to
[(0,a_{1}),\dots(n-1,a_{n})]
demonstrating that
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
refines
\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
.

4.
FVM theorems for counting logic

We now consider another relationship induced by a game comonad. To do so, we first introduce one of the two standard categories associated with any comonad.

4.1.
Kleisli category

Given a comonad
\mathbb{C}
on a category
\mathcal{C}
, the
Kleisli category

[
Kle65
]

\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})
for
\mathbb{C}
on
\mathcal{C}
is a category which

•

has the same objects as
\mathcal{C}
, and

•

the morphisms of type
A\rightarrow B
in
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})
are the morphisms of type
\mathbb{C}(A)\to B
in
\mathcal{C}
. To avoid ambiguity, we shall write
f:A\to_{\mathbb{C}}B
if we intend
f
to be understood as a Kleisli morphism.

•

For an object
A
, the identity morphism
A\to_{\mathbb{C}}A
is the counit component
\varepsilon_{A}:\mathbb{C}(A)\rightarrow A
.

•

The composite of morphisms
f\colon A\to_{\mathbb{C}}B
and
g\colon B\to_{\mathbb{C}}C
is given by
g\circ f^{*}
, where we recall
f^{*}:\mathbb{C}(A)\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(B)
is the coextension of
f\colon\mathbb{C}(A)\to B
. As composition is different to that in the base category, we use the distinct notation
g\mathbin{\bullet}f
for the Kleisli composite of
f
and
g
.

The axioms for a comonad in Kleisli form ensure that this is a well-defined category. Notice that the morphisms in the Kleisli category are exactly those that were important for positive existential fragments in Section
3
.

The motivation for the Kleisli construction is that there is an obvious forgetful functor
U_{\mathbb{C}}:\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, and this functor has an identity on objects right adjoint
cofree functor

F_{\mathbb{C}}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})
, and this adjunction induces the original comonad.

As with any category, we can consider the isomorphisms in
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})
. We shall write

A\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}B

if structures
A
and
B
are isomorphic in the Kleisli category. For finite structures, the relations
A\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
and
A\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B
correspond to equivalence in counting logic
[
ADW17
,
AS21
]
. The ideas of Theorem
3
can be extended to establish an FVM theorem for counting fragments and the
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}
relation. All that is required is to impose extra conditions on the collection of morphisms (
4
). Furthermore, these extra conditions turn out to be those of the well-known Kleisli laws related to lifting functors to Kleisli categories
[
MM07
]
.

To motivate our comonadic method, we return to the minimal reduct example from Section
3
. We can show that
A\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
implies
A|_{\tau}\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B|_{\tau}
,
via a similar ad-hoc argument to before.
In our setting, we wish to establish that, for mutually inverse morphisms

f\colon A\to_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
and
g\colon B\to_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}A
in
\mathsf{Kl}({\mathbb{E}_{k}})
,
the composites
f|_{\tau}\circ\kappa_{A}
and
g|_{\tau}\circ\kappa_{B}
, constructed as in (
3
), are also mutually inverse in the Kleisli category.
In the following we analyse the abstract setting in which the relation
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}
is defined and derive a general result, similar to Theorem
3
.

4.2.
Kleisli laws

Akin to our motivating example, we assume a unary operation
H:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
,
comonads
(\mathbb{C},\varepsilon^{\mathbb{C}},(-)^{*})
on
\mathcal{C}
and
(\mathbb{D},\varepsilon^{\mathbb{D}},(-)^{*})
on
\mathcal{D}
, and also a collection of morphisms

\{\kappa_{A}\colon\mathbb{D}H(A)\to H\mathbb{C}(A)\mid A\in\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{C})\},

as in Theorem
3
. We wish to find conditions on this collection of morphisms ensuring that
A\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}B
implies
H(A)\equiv_{\#\mathbb{D}}H(B)
. Rephrasing, we require axioms such that if
f\colon A\to_{\mathbb{C}}B
and
g\colon B\to_{\mathbb{C}}A
are mutually inverse, then the composites

H(f)\circ\kappa_{A}\colon H(A)\to_{\mathbb{D}}H(B)\quad\mbox{and}\quad H(g)\circ\kappa_{B}\colon H(B)\to_{\mathbb{D}}H(A)

are also mutually inverse. To do so, we observe that if both

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}H(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\mathbb{C}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{H(A)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\varepsilon_{A})}

(5)

and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}H(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}H(B)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\mathbb{C}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\mathbb{C}(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H(f)\circ\kappa_{A})^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(f^{*})}

(6)

commute then, from
g\mathbin{\bullet}f=\mathrm{id}
in
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D})
(i.e.
g\circ f^{*}=\varepsilon_{A}
), we obtain that

\displaystyle(H(g)\circ\kappa_{B})\mathbin{\bullet}(H(f)\circ\kappa_{A})

\displaystyle=H(g)\circ\kappa_{B}\circ(H(f)\circ\kappa_{A})^{*}

definition of
\mathbin{\bullet}

\displaystyle=H(g)\circ H(f^{*})\circ\kappa_{A}

(
6
)

\displaystyle=H(g\circ f^{*})\circ\kappa_{A}

functoriality

\displaystyle=H(\varepsilon_{A})\circ\kappa_{A}

assumption

\displaystyle=\varepsilon_{H(A)}:\mathbb{D}(H(A))\to H(A)

(
5
)

and, similarly,

f\mathbin{\bullet}g=\mathrm{id}
implies that

(H(f)\circ\kappa_{A})\mathbin{\bullet}(H(g)\circ\kappa_{B})

equals to identity in
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D})
.
In fact, equations (
5
) and (
6
) are equivalent to requiring that the morphisms
\kappa_{A}
constitute a natural transformation
\kappa:\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\mathbb{C}
making the following two diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\circ H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\circ\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\varepsilon}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\circ H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}^{2}\circ H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\circ H\circ\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\circ\mathbb{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\circ\mathbb{C}^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa_{\mathbb{C}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\delta}

(7)

Such a
\kappa
is referred to as a
Kleisli law

[
MM07
]
.

Lemma 5
.

A collection of morphisms
\{\kappa_{A}\}
satisfies equations (
5
) and (
6
) for every
f\colon\mathbb{C}(A)\to B
iff
\kappa
is a Kleisli law.

Proof 4.1
.

For the left to right implication,
\kappa
is natural since, for any
g\colon A\to B
, we set
f=\mathbb{C}(A)\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{A}}A\xrightarrow{g}B
and observe that
H(\mathbb{C}(g))=H(f^{*})
and
\mathbb{D}(H(g))=(H(g)\circ\varepsilon_{H(A)})^{*}=(H(f)\circ\kappa)^{*}
by (
5
). Naturality then follows from (
6
). Commutativity of the oblong in (
7
) follows by setting
f=\mathbb{C}(A)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}\mathbb{C}(A)
. We then have
(H(f)\circ\kappa_{A})^{*}=\mathbb{D}(\kappa_{A})\circ\delta_{H(A)}
and
\kappa_{A}\circ\mathbb{D}(\kappa_{A})\circ\delta_{H(A)}=H(f^{*})\circ\kappa_{A}=H(\delta_{A})\circ\kappa_{A}
from which the result follows by (
5
).

Conversely, for an
f\colon\mathbb{C}(A)\to B
, by the oblong law in (
7
) and naturality of
\kappa
,
H(f^{*})\circ\kappa_{A}=H(\mathbb{C}(f))\circ\kappa_{\mathbb{C}(A)}\circ\mathbb{D}(\kappa_{A})\circ\delta_{H(A)}=\kappa_{B}\circ\mathbb{D}(H(f))\circ\mathbb{D}(\kappa_{A})\circ\delta_{H(A)}=\kappa_{B}\circ(H(f)\circ\kappa_{A})^{*}
.

Remark 6
.

Kleisli laws for monads given by a collection of morphisms satisfying (
5
) and (
6
) appeared in the study of relative monads
[
Ahr12
,
Ahr15
]
, under the name
colax morphisms
.
Kleisli laws correspond precisely to liftings (defined as Kleisli liftings below) of the operation
H\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
to operations
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D})
commuting with the cofree functors
\mathcal{C}\to\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathcal{D}\to\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D})
. See for example
[
Mul93
,
Jac94
]
.
Furthermore, a Kleisli law
\kappa:\mathbb{C}\Rightarrow\mathbb{D}
with respect to the identity functor (as the operation) is the same thing as a
comonad morphism
.

We will typically be interested in operations of arity greater than one, so we immediately extend our definitions.

Remark 7

(Notation)
.

For a set
I
, and family of categories
\{\mathcal{C}_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, we shall write

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil

for a family of objects, with each
A_{i}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
, and similarly

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle f_{i}\hfil

for families of morphisms. These form the
product category

\prod_{i\in I}\mathcal{C}_{i}
, with composition and identities defined componentwise. Given a family of comonads
\mathbb{C}_{i}:\mathcal{C}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{i}
, they induce a comonad
\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
on the product category, again componentwise.

Using this notation, we define the
n
-ary version of the axioms from Lemma
5
.
Kleisli laws
for an
n
-ary operation
H
are the natural transformations
\kappa\colon\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
such that the following diagrams commute:

(K1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\circ H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\prod_{i}\varepsilon)}

(K2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\circ H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}^{2}\circ H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\circ H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}^{2}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\prod_{i}\delta)}

In fact,
n
-ary Kleisli laws are a special case of ordinary (unary) Kleisli laws, as will be shown in Lemma
8
.

For a family of comonads
\mathbb{C}_{i}:\mathcal{C}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
we will say that a functor
H^{\prime}:\prod_{i}\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\rightarrow\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D}_{i})

is a
Kleisli lifting
of functor
H:\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}

if the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathsf{Kl}(\mathcal{C}_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}F_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\mathbb{D}}}

(8)

Lemma 8
.

Given a family of comonads
\mathbb{C}_{i}:\mathcal{C}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{i}
:

(1)

The Kleisli construction commutes with products, in that the following equation holds

\mathsf{Kl}(\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i})=\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C}_{i})

(2)

The forgetful functor and cofree functors of the product comonad

U_{\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}}:\mathsf{Kl}(\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i})\rightarrow\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\qquad F_{\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}}:\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\rightarrow\mathsf{Kl}(\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i})

are given by the product
\prod_{i}U_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}
of forgetful and product
\prod_{i}F_{\mathbb{C}_{i}}
of cofree functors, respectively.

(3)

An n-ary Kleisli laws of type
\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
is the same thing as a (unary) Kleisli law involving the product comonad
\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
.

(4)

n-ary Kleisli laws bijectively correspond to Kleisli liftings of
H
.

Proof 4.2
.

Items
1
and
2
are routine calculations from the definitions.
Item
3
follows directly from the definitions,
and so we can reduce the bijective relationship between n-ary Kleisli laws and Kleisli liftings to the well-known unary case. See for example
[
Mul93
]
. This establishes item
4
.

The argument presented for a unary opereration generalises smoothly to operations of any arity, yielding an FVM theorem for counting fragments.

Theorem 9
.

Let
\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n}
and
\mathbb{D}
be comonads on categories
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
\mathcal{D}
, respectively, and

H\colon\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}

a functor.
If there exists a Kleisli law of type

\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}

then

A_{1}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}_{1}}B_{1}\;\ldots,\;A_{n}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}_{n}}B_{n}

implies

H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})\;\equiv_{\#\mathbb{D}}\;H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n}).

Proof 4.3
.

Assume
A_{1}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}_{1}}B_{1}
,  …,
A_{n}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}_{n}}B_{n}
or, equivalently,

F_{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(A_{1})\cong F_{C_{1}}(B_{1}),\kern 5.0pt\ldots,\kern 5.0ptF_{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(A_{n})\cong F_{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(B_{n}).

The assumed Kleisli law induces a Kleisli lifting
H^{\prime}\colon\prod_{i}\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\rightarrow\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D}_{i})
by item
4
of Lemma
8
, which by functoriality implies

H^{\prime}(F_{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(A_{1}),\ldots,F_{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(A_{n}))\cong H^{\prime}(F_{C_{1}}(B_{1}),\ldots,F_{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(B_{n})).

By the commutativity of (
8
) we have

F_{\mathbb{D}}(H(A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}))\cong F_{\mathbb{D}}(H(B_{1},\ldots,B_{n}))
,
which encodes the required property.

4.3.
Examples

{exa}

The collection of
\kappa
morphisms described in each of
Examples
3.1
,
3.1
and
3.1
of Section
3.1

are natural and satisfy axioms
(K1)
and
(K2)
.
Therefore, by applying Theorem
9
to the Kleisli laws in
Examples
3.1
and
3.1

we have that
\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
and
\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
are preserved by taking coproducts of structures and the merge operation maps
\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}
to
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{M}_{k+1}}
.
Recalling Remark
6
, by applying Theorem
9
to the comonad morphism in Example
3.1
we have that for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{P}_{2}}
refines
\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}
, and
\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
refines
\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
.

{exa}

[Cospectrality]
Two graphs
G
and
H
are
cospectral
if the adjacency matrices of
G
and
H
have the same multiset of eigenvalues.
We shall use Theorem
9
to strengthen a result in
[
DSZ17
]
showing that equivalence in 3-variable counting logic
with equality
implies cospectrality.

Since cospectrality is a notion on graphs, we move to the full subcategory of loopless undirected graphs in signature
\sigma=\{E\}
where
E
is a binary edge relation.
The comonad
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
restricts to this category and we also consider a comonad
\mathtt{Cos}
defined in
[
AJP22
, Section 3]
which characterizes cospectrality,
in that

G\equiv_{\#\mathtt{Cos}}H
is equivalent to
G
and
H
being cospectral.
The universe of
\mathtt{Cos}(G)
can be encoded as pairs
(c,v_{i})
where
c
is a closed walk
2
2

2

Recall that a
walk
is a sequence of (possibly repeated vertices) where
v_{j}
is adjacent to
v_{j+1}
.

v_{0}\xrightarrow{E}v_{1}\xrightarrow{E}\ldots\xrightarrow{E}v_{n}\xrightarrow{E}v_{0}
that passes through
v_{i}
.
Two pairs
(c,v_{i}),(c^{\prime},v_{j})
are adjacent in
\mathtt{Cos}(G)
if
c=c^{\prime}
and
v_{i}
is adjacent to
v_{j}
in the closed walk
c
.
The counit
\varepsilon_{G}
maps
(c,v_{i})
to
v_{i}
.
For
h\colon\mathtt{Cos}(G)\rightarrow H
, the coextension
h^{*}
maps the pair
(c,v_{i})
with
c
being the closed walk
v_{0}\xrightarrow{E}v_{1}\xrightarrow{E}\ldots\xrightarrow{E}v_{n}\xrightarrow{E}v_{0}
to
(d,h(c,v_{i}))
where
d
is the closed walk
h(c,v_{0})\xrightarrow{E}h(c,v_{1})\xrightarrow{E}\ldots\xrightarrow{E}h(c,v_{n})\xrightarrow{E}h(c,v_{0})
.

We define a comonad morphism
\kappa\colon\mathtt{Cos}(G)\to\mathbb{P}_{3}(G)
where
(c,v_{i})
is mapped to the word

[(2,v_{0}),(1,v_{1}),(0,v_{2}),(1,v_{3}),\dots,(i\bmod 2,v_{i})].

Recalling Remark
6
, we apply Theorem
9
, deducing that
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{P}_{3}}
implies
\equiv_{\#\mathtt{Cos}}
.
Since
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{P}_{3}}
captures equivalence in 3-variable counting logic
without equality
and
\equiv_{\#\mathtt{Cos}}
captures cospectrality, we have avoided the need for equality in the logic.
This fact is also a consequence of
[
DJR21
, Theorem 32]
and the original theorem of Dawar, Severini, and Zapata
[
DSZ17
]
.
However, the same reasoning allows us to define a comonad morphism
\mathtt{Cos}\Rightarrow\mathbb{PR}_{3}
where
\mathbb{PR}_{3}
is the pebble-relation comonad from
[
MS22
]
, capturing the restricted conjunction fragment of
3
-variable counting logic.
The universe of
\mathbb{PR}_{3}(G)
consists of pairs
(s,i)
with
s\in\mathbb{P}_{3}(G)
is a sequence of length
n
and
i\in\{0,1,\dots,n\}
is an index into the sequence
s
.
In this case,
(c,v_{i})
is mapped to the pair
(s,i)
where
s=\kappa(c,v_{n})
.
Intuitively,
s
enumerates the entire closed walk
c
and the index
i
picks out
v_{i}
in this walk.
This proves a genuine strengthening of the previous result.

{exa}

The theses
[
Sha24
, Section 6.3.3]
and the forthcoming
[
Kar26
]
take a look at the quantum monad
\mathbf{Q}_{d}
from
[
ABdSZ17
]
on categories of structures with a distinguished ‘commeasurablity’ relation.
For each
d>0
,
\mathbf{Q}_{d}(A)
is a structure on
d
-dimensional projector-valued measurements of
A
, which is used for modeling perfect quantum strategies in a non-local homomorphism game.
Similarly, one can adapt the
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
comonad to the same category.
It is shown in the
op. cit.
(independently) that there is a Kleisli law
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}\circ\mathbf{Q}_{d}\Rightarrow\mathbf{Q}_{d}\circ{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
, giving us that the equivalence in the counting fragment
A\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
implies
\mathbf{Q}_{d}(A)\equiv_{\#{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}\mathbf{Q}_{d}(B)
.

5.
Coalgebras and open pathwise-embeddings

In Sections
3
and
4
we described FVM theorems for the equivalence relations
\equiv_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}}
and
\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}
, typically expressing logical equivalence for the positive existential and counting logic variants. To do the same for the full logic requires us to move from the Kleisli category to the richer setting of the
Eilenberg–Moore category of coalgebras
.

For a comonad
\mathbb{C}
on
\mathcal{C}
, a pair
(A,\alpha)
where
\alpha\colon A\to\mathbb{C}(A)
is a morphism in
\mathcal{C}
is a
\mathbb{C}
-coalgebra
or simply just
coalgebra
if the following two diagrams commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{A}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \mathbb{C}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(\mathbb{C}(A))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(\alpha)}

(9)

A
morphism of coalgebras

f\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
is a morphism
f\colon A\to B
such that
\beta\circ f=\mathbb{C}(f)\circ\alpha
. We write
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
for the Eilenberg–Moore category of coalgebras of
\mathbb{C}
.

The original motivation for the Eilenberg-Moore construction is that there is an obvious forgetful functor
U^{\mathbb{C}}:\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
, mapping
(A,\alpha)
to
A
, and it has a right adjoint
cofree coalgebra
functor
F^{\mathbb{C}}:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
, such that the adjunction
U^{\mathbb{C}}\dashv F^{\mathbb{C}}
induces the comonad
\mathbb{C}
. We will need the cofree construction in later developments. Concretely its action on objects is
F^{\mathbb{C}}(A)=(\mathbb{C}(A),\delta_{A})
.

Remark 10
.

A coalgebra
(A,\alpha)
of any of the game comonads defined in Section
2.2
is endowed with a preorder
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
, where
x\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}y
whenever the word
\alpha(x)
is a prefix of
\alpha(y)
.
A
forest order
is a poset in which the set of predecessors of any element in a finite chain. It follows from the coalgebra axioms in (
9
) that
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
is a forest order on the universe. Furthermore, one can check that
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
is
compatible
with the relational structure of
A
. This means that
R^{A}(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})
implies that for every
i,j
either
a_{i}\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}a_{j}
or
a_{j}\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}a_{i}
.

In fact
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{E}_{k}})
is equivalent to the category of
\sigma
-structures endowed with a compatible forest order of depth
{\leq}\,k
. Similar characterisations of the categories
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{P}_{k}})
and
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{M}_{k}})
can be made as well, cf.
[
AS21
, Section 9]
.

We are now almost ready to define the relation
\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Given
A,B\in\mathcal{C}
, define
A\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}B

if there exists a span of
open pathwise-embeddings

F^{\mathbb{C}}(A)\leftarrow Z\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{C}}(B).

We postpone the definition of open pathwise-embeddings to Section
5.2
.

In terms of our example comonads,
A\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
and
A\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B
correspond to agreement on first-order sentences of
k
-bounded quantifier depth and variable count respectively
[
AS21
]
. Similarly,
(A,a)\equiv_{{\mathbb{M}_{k}}}(B,b)
characterises agreement on modal formulae of modal depth at most
k
.

Returning again to the example of reducts, showing the trivial fact that
A\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
implies
A|_{\tau}\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B|_{\tau}
in our setting suggests the strategy:

(1)

Lift an
n
-ary operation
H
to the level of coalgebras, in a manner that commutes with the construction
F^{\mathbb{C}}(-)
of cofree coalgebras.

(2)

Check that open pathwise-embeddings are preserved by the lifted operation.

Tackling step
1
is the topic of Section
5.1
. Step
2
is the subject of the subsequent Section
5.2
.

5.1.
Lifting operations to coalgebras

Here we focus on the task of lifting a given operation to the level of coalgebras.
By an
Eilenberg–Moore lifting
, or just
lifting
in short, of a functor
H:\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\rightarrow\mathcal{D}
we mean a functor

\widehat{H}:\prod_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\rightarrow\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
such that the following diagram commutes
up to isomorphism:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}F^{\mathbb{C}_{i}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathbb{D}}}

(10)

To this end, we generalise a standard technique from monad theory, used for lifting adjunctions to the Eilenberg–Moore categories
[
Joh75
]
or to lifting monoidal structure to the algebras of commutative monad
[
Koc71b
]
(see also
[
Jac94
,
Sea13
,
JMS22b
]
).
For such a lifting to exist, given a Kleisli law, it is sufficient that the codomain category of coalgebras has sufficient equalisers.
In our case, it is enough to check that the comonad on the target category preserves embeddings and the rest is ensured by Linton’s theorem
[
Lin69
]
.
In the following paragraphs we review the terminology necessary for the said theorem.

We say that a pair of classes of morphisms
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
is a
weak factorisation system
in a category
\mathcal{C}
if:

•

Every morphism
f
in
\mathcal{C}
factorises as
f=m\circ e
for some
m\in\mathcal{M}
and
e\in\mathcal{E}
.

•

\mathcal{E}=\{e\mid\forall m\in\mathcal{M},e\pitchfork m\}
and
\mathcal{M}=\{m\mid\forall e\in\mathcal{E},e\pitchfork m\}
, where
e\pitchfork m
expresses that
e
is weakly orthogonal to
m
, that is, for any commutative diagram as shown on the left-hand side below

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

there exists a diagonal morphism
d
as shown in the diagram on the right-hand side above, making the diagram commute.

Morphisms of
\mathcal{E}
are called
quotients
and are denoted by
\twoheadrightarrow
and morphisms of
\mathcal{M}
are
embeddings
which we denote by
\rightarrowtail
.
We further say that
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
is a
proper factorisation system
if it is a weak factorisation system such that
\mathcal{E}
is a class of epimorphisms and
\mathcal{M}
is a class of monomorphisms.

{exa}

Factorisation systems are omnipresent in category theory. For example, the category of sets is equipped with the (surjectives, injectives) proper factorisation system. Similarly,
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
come equipped with
(\text{epis},\text{strong monos})
factorisation system
[
AR94
]
. In this case epis are precisely the surjective homomorphisms of (pointed)
\sigma
-structures and strong monos are precisely the
embeddings
, that is, injective homomorphisms
f\colon A\to B
which reflect relations.
In fact, strong monos are the same as regular, extremal, or effective monos in this
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
.

In the following, we always assume that (pointed)
\sigma
-structures come equipped with (epi, strong mono) factorisation system.

Finally, for a comonad
\mathbb{C}
on a category
\mathcal{C}
with a proper factorisation system we say that
\mathbb{C}

preserves embeddings
if
\mathbb{C}(f)
is an embedding whenever
f
is. In similar fashion, an
n
-ary operation
H
preserves embeddings if
H(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
is an embedding whenever
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
are embeddings in their respective categories.

Theorem 11

(Linton
[
Lin69
]
)
.

Given a comonad
\mathbb{C}
on a category
\mathcal{C}
equipped with a proper factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
if

(1)

\mathbb{C}
preserves
\mathcal{M}
-morphisms,

(2)

\mathcal{C}
has coproducts and

(3)

\mathcal{C}
is
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered
, that is, every object
A
of
\mathcal{C}
has, up to isomorphism, only a set of embeddings
X\rightarrowtail A
.

then
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
has equalisers.

Remark 12
.

This is essentially the dual of
[
Lin69
, Proposition 4]
, up to some small technical differences. We provide a full proof in the form we require for completeness.

To prove Linton’s theorem, we first establish some preliminaries. We need a standard fact that comonads that preserve embeddings lift the factorisation system from the underlying category to their category of coalgebras. This is essential not only for the proof of Theorem
11
but also for the definition of
\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}
in Section
5.2
.

Lemma 13
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a comonad on a category
\mathcal{C}
with a proper factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
. If
\mathbb{C}
preserves embeddings then
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
admits a proper factorisation system
(\overline{\mathcal{E}},\overline{\mathcal{M}})
where a morphism of coalgebras
h\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
is in
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
(resp.
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
) if the underlying morphism
h\colon A\to B
is in
\mathcal{E}
(resp.
\mathcal{M}
).

Proof 5.1
.

The statement is analogous to that of Lemma 2 in
[
Lin69
]
, except that we do not require functoriality of factorisations which, however, plays no role in the proof.

Next, we prove an intermediate statement from which Linton’s theorem follows. In the proof of Theorem
11
the category
\mathcal{A}
will be
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
for some comonad
\mathbb{C}
.

Lemma 14
.

If a category
\mathcal{A}
has a proper factorization system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
, is
\mathcal{M}
-well-powered and has coproducts then
\mathcal{A}
has equalisers.

Proof 5.2
.

Consider parallel pair:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

As
\mathcal{A}
is well-powered and has a factorization system, there is a set
I
of isomorphism classes
[m_{i}]
of embeddings
m_{i}:M_{i}\rightarrow A
equalizing
f
and
g
. As

f\circ[m_{i}]=[f\circ m_{i}]=[g\circ m_{i}]=g\circ[m_{i}]

the induced morphism
\widetilde{m}:\coprod_{i}M_{i}\to A
equalises
f
and
g
.
As
\mathcal{A}
has a factorization system, the morphism
\widetilde{m}
factors as:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{i}M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}

Next, assume
h:C\rightarrow A
equalises
f
and
g
. Observe that
m^{\prime}
in the
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
factorisation
m^{\prime}\circ e^{\prime}
of
h
also equalises
f
and
g
since
e^{\prime}
is an epimorphism. We may assume that
m^{\prime}=m_{i}
for some
i
.
Let
j
be the corresponding coproduct injection. The following commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{i}M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

As the factorization system is proper,
m
is a monomorphism, and so
e\circ j\circ e^{\prime}
is the unique morphism
C\to E
factorising
h
via
m
.
Consequently,
m
equalises
f
and
g
.

We’re now ready to prove Linton’s theorem.

Proof 5.3

(Proof of Theorem
11
.)
.

We check that
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
14
.
By Lemma
13
, since
\mathbb{C}
preserves embeddings the proper factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
on
\mathcal{C}
lifts to a proper factorisation system
(\overline{\mathcal{E}},\overline{\mathcal{M}})
on
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
. By the definition of
\overline{\mathcal{M}}
since
\mathcal{C}
is well-powered so is
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
. Finally,
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
has coproducts since the forgetful functor
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathcal{C}
reflects them.

All the comonads that we have introduced thus far preserve embeddings and
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
and
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
both have coproducts. In the case of
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
these are disjoint unions of structures and in the case of
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
we glue the disjoint union by the distinguished elements. Finally, we see that both categories are also well-powered because each embedding
X\rightarrowtail A
is determined by its image in
A
and there are only set-many of these.

Corollary 15
.

If a comonad
\mathbb{C}
on
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
or on
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
preserves embeddings then
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
has equalisers.
In particular,
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{E}_{k}})
,
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{P}_{k}})
and
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{M}_{k}})
have equalisers.

Let us come back to the question of lifting functors
H\colon\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}
to the category of coalgebras, in sense of (
10
) above. It might come as a surprise that nothing more than a Kleisli law and existence of equalisers is needed for the lift to exist.

Theorem 16
.

If
\kappa
is a Kleisli law of type
\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
and
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
has equalisers then the lifting of
H
to
\widehat{H}:\prod_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\rightarrow\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
exists.

Remark 17

(Notation)
.

In the following we often just write
\alpha
instead of
(A,\alpha)
in expressions that involve a coalgebra
(A,\alpha)\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
. In particular, the morphism
\iota_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
below is parametrised by the coalgebras
\{\,(A_{i},\alpha_{i})\,\}_{i}
and the same is true for the resulting coalgebra
\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\alpha_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})
.

Furthermore, we often omit the subscripts for the components of natural transformations, if they can be easily inferred from the context. For example, we shall write
\kappa
instead of
\kappa_{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}}
and
\lambda_{H}
instead of the more verbose
\lambda_{H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})}
.

, for

is a lifting of

be a collection of objects, for

Remark 18
.

Observe that (
11
) is a coreflexive equaliser, i.e. it is an equaliser of a parallel pair of morphisms with a common retraction (post-inverse). The common retraction of the parallel pair in (
11
) is given by
\mathbb{D}H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\varepsilon_{A_{i}}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern Remark 19
.

Appendix A of
[
BK11
]
contains a statement analogous to our Theorem
16
above. In fact the authors show that in case of a unary functor
H\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
, the lifting
\widehat{H}\colon\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
is the right Kan extension of
K_{\mathbb{D}}\circ H^{\prime}
along
K_{\mathbb{C}}
where
H^{\prime}
is the Kleisli lifting
\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{D})
from (
8
) and
K_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\mathsf{Kl}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
is the comparison functor.

5.2.
Open pathwise-embeddings and their preservation

In this section we define open pathwise-embeddings and give sufficient conditions for the lifted functors of Section
5.1
to preserve them. In fact, our conditions are a combination of two other categorical properties: parametric adjoints and relative adjoints, which we discuss in Appendix
A
.

To emphasise the main ingredients of our arguments we restrict to a simplified setting of
path categories
. These are categories
\mathcal{A}
equipped with a proper factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
and a choice of
path
objects
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{A})
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

In this setting, an embedding
e\colon P\rightarrowtail X
is a
path embedding
if
P
is a path (i.e.
P\in\mathcal{P}
). Then,
f\colon X\to Y
is
pathwise-embedding
if
f\circ e
is an embedding for every path embedding
e\colon P\rightarrowtail X
. Finally,
f
is
open
if for every
commutative square of solid arrows as shown on the right,
where
e,m,g
are path embeddings,
there exists a morphism
d\colon Q\to X
such that
e=d\circ g
and
m=f\circ d
.

{exa}

Recall from Remark
10
that coalgebras
(A,\alpha)
, for either of our example comonad
\mathbb{C}\in\{{\mathbb{E}_{k}},{\mathbb{P}_{k}},{\mathbb{M}_{k}}\}
, come equipped with a forest order
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
on the universe. In the following, we always assume that paths
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
are the coalgebras
(A,\alpha)
which are finite linear orders in
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}
.

Also, since
\mathbb{C}
typically preserves embeddings of
\sigma
-structures,
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
admits a proper factorisation system
(\overline{\mathcal{E}},\overline{\mathcal{M}})
by lifting the (epi, strong mono) factorisation system of (pointed)
\sigma
-structures, cf. Lemma
13
.
Consequently, we view
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{E}_{k}})
,
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{P}_{k}})
, and
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{M}_{k}})
as path categories in the above sense.
In fact, every
arboreal category
, in sense of
[
AR21
]
, is a path category. Note that the notion of path category in
[
AR21
]
is different from ours.

Furthermore, coalgebra morphisms
f\colon(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
, for any comonad
\mathbb{C}
from
\{{\mathbb{E}_{k}},{\mathbb{P}_{k}},{\mathbb{M}_{k}}\}
, are homomorphisms of the underlying structures
A\to B
which respect the forest orders. The latter means that
f
is a poset morphism (i.e.
a\sqsubseteq_{\alpha}a^{\prime}
implies
f(a)\sqsubseteq_{\beta}f(a^{\prime})
) which, furthermore, sends elements of height 1 (i.e. roots) to elements of height 1, elements of height 2 to elements of height 2, and so on. In case of
\mathbb{C}={\mathbb{P}_{k}}
or
\mathbb{C}={\mathbb{M}_{k}}
the additional tree decorations such as the pebble index or binary relations need to be preserved too.
Now,
f
is a pathwise-embedding precisely whenever any restriction of
f
along a finite branch is an embedding. Finally,
f
is open whenever
f
is a bounded morphism (in sense of modal logic) with respect to the tree orders.

Remark 20
.

With the notion of open pathwise-embedding for
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
with paths and embeddings specified as above, we recall that

A\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}B
iff there exists a pair of open pathwise-embeddings
F^{\mathbb{C}}(A)\leftarrow Z\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{C}}(B)
, where
F^{\mathbb{C}}(A)
is the cofree coalgebra on
A
.

We now come back to our original goal. In order for an
n
-ary operation
H\colon\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}
to admit an FVM theorem, we wish to show that its lifting

\widehat{H}\colon\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})

described in Theorem
16
, sends tuples of open pathwise-embeddings to open pathwise-embeddings.

In our simplified setting, we have a functor
\widehat{H}\colon\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\mathcal{B}
between path categories.
In the remainder of this section show that
\widehat{H}
preserves open pathwise-embeddings assuming the following two conditions:

(S1)

\widehat{H}\colon\prod_{i}\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\mathcal{B}
preserves embeddings.

(S2)

2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu)
,
for some path embeddings
g_{i}\colon Q_{i}\rightarrowtail A_{i}
, there exist necessarily unique morphisms
h_{i}\colon P_{i}\to Q_{i}
such that
e_{i}=g_{i}\circ h_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,n
.

We are going to need the following standard facts about embeddings of a proper factorisation system. The dual statement for quotients holds too but we do not need it here.

Lemma 21

(e.g. Section 2 in
[
FK72
]
)
.

For a category with a proper factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
:

(1)

All equalisers are embeddings.

(2)

Embeddings are closed under composition.

(3)

If
g\circ f
is an embedding then so is
f
.

These basic properties of embeddings listed in Lemma
21
allow us to show the first half of our desired result, preservation of pathwise-embeddings.

Lemma 22
.

If
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
are pathwise-embeddings in
\mathcal{A}_{1}
, …,
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, respectively, then so is
\widehat{H}(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
.

Proof 5.5
.

Assume
e\colon P\rightarrowtail\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern is a path embedding where, for each

is the domain of

. By

,

decomposes as:

.3,

is an embedding because

is. Further, since

is a pathwise-embedding, for

is an embedding. Therefore, by
(S1)
,
\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern For the preservation of openness, we need the following technical lemma.

Lemma 23
.

in

be pathwise-embeddings, for each

and

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\scriptstyle\hfil\textstyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}{\vbox{\kern-0.7pt\cr$\scriptstyle\mkern {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}

Then, for
i=1,\dots,n
, there exist morphisms

f^{\prime}_{i}\colon P_{i}\to Q_{i},

such that
the diagram on the right above commutes.

and

are the minimal embeddings given by

such that

and

decompose through

the morphism

is an embedding and since embeddings are monomorphisms, we obtain that

.

Consequently, we obtain another decomposition of
e
, given by
\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern
