. By minimality

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle e_{i}\hfil

, there exists

f^{\prime}_{i}\colon P_{i}\to Q_{i},

for
i=1,\dots,n
, such that
e_{i}=e_{i}\circ l_{i}\circ f^{\prime}_{i}
. Therefore, for
i=1,\dots,n
, we see that

f_{i}\circ e_{i}=f_{i}\circ e_{i}\circ l_{i}\circ f^{\prime}_{i}=g_{i}\circ f^{\prime}_{i},

which concludes the proof.

We are now ready to proof the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 24
.

If
\widehat{H}
satisfies
(S1)
and
(S2)
and
f_{1},\dots,f_{n}
are open pathwise-embeddings, then so is
\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu)
.

Proof 5.7
.

Given for
i=1,\dots,n
, open pathwise-embeddings
f_{i}\colon A_{i}\to B_{i}
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})
, it is enough to check that

Then, by minimality of this decomposition and by Lemma

, for

, there exist

and

such that

and

. Since

is open and

, there is a morphism

such that
d_{i}\circ h_{i}\circ f^{\prime}_{i}=e_{i}
and
g_{i}=d_{i}\circ f_{i}
. Finally, because the outer rectangle and the bottom rectangle commute and
\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern is the required diagonal filler of the outer square.

6.
FVM theorems for the full logic

Theorems
16
and
24
above can be used to state an abstract FVM theorem for full fragments.
However, in order to use such theorem in concrete instances we need to be able to check its assumptions easily.
To this end, we rephrase axioms
(S1)
and
(S2)
in more elementary terms.
The key ingredient is the notion of heteromorphism which allows us to understand morphisms involved in axiom
(S2)
in terms of the original operation and underlying categories.

6.1.
Heteromorphisms

Recall from linear algebra that, for vector spaces
U,V,W
, a bilinear map (or bimorphism) is a function
f\colon U\times V\to W
such that, for any
u\in U
and
v\in V
the functions
f(u,-)
and
f(-,v)
are linear. The universal property of bimorphisms states that bilinear maps
U\times V\to W
are in a bijective correspondence with (ordinary) linear maps
U\otimes V\to W
, where
\otimes
denotes the tensor product operation on vector spaces.
This is an instance of a general phenomenon from the theory of monads, that is, the duals of comonads. Concretely, if a monad
T
is
commutative
(i.e. there is a Kleisli law
T(A)\times T(B)\to T(A\times B)
satisfying extra conditions
[
Koc71b
]
) then the category of algebras for
T
has a tensor operation
\otimes
, which is the lifting of the product operation
\times
(in dual sense to our Section
5.1
). Moreover, algebra homomorphisms
A\otimes B\to C
correspond to certain bilinear morphisms
A\times B\to C

[
Koc71a
]
.

In what follows, we often need to analyse morphisms of the form
(A,\alpha)\to\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle(B_{i},\beta_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle(B_{i},\beta_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle(B_{i},\beta_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle(B_{i},\beta_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}})
, for some coalgebras
(A,\alpha),(B_{1},\beta_{1}),\dots,(B_{n},\beta_{n})
. To aid with this we restate a direct generalisation of the universal property of bilinear maps and
[
Koc71a
]
to our setting.
Intuitively, instead of algebra homomorphisms
A\otimes B\to C
we study coalgebra morphisms
(A,\alpha)\to\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern heteromorphism
3
3

3

The name originates in
[
Ell06
]
where it was used to talk about morphisms between objects of different categories. These usually take the form
A\to F(B)
where
F
is a functor. In our case we further require the condition (
14
) enforced by the Kleisli law.
.

\kappa:\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\scriptstyle\hfil\textstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}{\vbox{\kern-0.7pt\cr$\scriptstyle\mkern \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{D}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

(14)

In fact, Proposition
27
below establishes that the mapping
f\mapsto f^{\#}
gives a bijection between coalgebra morphisms and heteromorphisms.
First we observe that heteromorphisms are closed under composition by coalgebra morphisms from both left and right.

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu}\circ f^{+}=\mathbb{D}(f)\circ\alpha
. Consequently,
\mathrm{u}_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu}\circ f^{+}=\varepsilon_{H}\circ\mathbb{D}(f)\circ\alpha=f\circ\varepsilon\circ\alpha=f
.

Item 3 follows from the fact that
\varepsilon
is a natural transformation and from the definition of
\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle f_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu)
in terms of the universal property of equalisers, cf. (
12
) above.

We are now ready to prove the promised statement.

Proof 6.2
.

Let
f\colon(A,\alpha)\to\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle(B_{i},\beta_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle(B_{i},\beta_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle(B_{i},\beta_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}})
be a coalgebra morphism.
Since
\mathrm{u}_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu}
is a heteromorphism (by Lemma
26
.1), so is
f^{\#}
by Lemma
25
. Furthermore,
(f^{\#})^{+}=f
by the universal property of
\mathrm{u}_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu}
(Lemma
26
.2). On the other hand, for a heteromorphism
g\colon\alpha\to[\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\beta_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern we clearly have

, again by the universal property of
\mathrm{u}_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern .

Lastly, we show that the mapping
f\mapsto f^{\#}
commutes with compositions of coalgebra morphisms.

Proposition 28
.

For a
\mathbb{D}
-coalgebra morphism
h\colon(A^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})\to(A,\alpha)
and
\mathbb{C}_{i}
-coalgebra morphisms
g_{i}\colon(B_{i},\beta_{i})\to(B^{\prime}_{i},\beta^{\prime}_{i})
:

(\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle g_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle g_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu)\circ f^{\#}

where the equality in the middle follows from Lemma
26
.3. We see that
(\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle g_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle g_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle g_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle g_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle g_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu)\circ f^{\#}\circ h
now follows from these two observations and Lemma
25
, which ensures that
f\circ h
is a heteromorphism.

6.2.
Simplifying the axioms

Axioms
(S1)
and
(S2)
give us sufficient conditions for functors between arbitrary path categories to preserve open pathwise-embeddings.
In our case the path categories of interests are categories of coalgebras for our game comonads and the functor in question
\widehat{H}
is typically a lifted functor
H
on the base categories. In this section we describe more elementary conditions, written in terms of heteromorphisms, that imply axioms
(S1)
and
(S2)
.
This way we can easily establish the validity of assumptions of our FVM theorems for full logic (Theorem
33
below), without having to analyse the lifted functor
\widehat{H}
.

In this section we fix a functor between base categories
H\colon\prod_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}
and assume that it lifts to
\widehat{H}\colon\prod_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
as in Theorem
16
.
To begin with, we observe that
(S1)
is implied by a similar property of
H
.

Proposition 29
.

If
H
preserves embeddings then so does
\widehat{H}
.

Proof 6.4
.

Let

e_{1}\colon(A_{1},\alpha_{1})\to(B_{1},\beta_{1}),\ \dots,\ e_{n}\colon(A_{n},\alpha_{n})\to(B_{n},\beta_{n})

be embeddings in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{1}),\dots,\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{n})
, respectively. Recall that
\widehat{H}(e_{1},\dots,e_{n})
is defined by the universal property of equalisers. In particular, it makes the following diagram commute.

To proceed we need the following simple lemma about decompositions of heteromorphisms.

Lemma 30
.

Assume that both
\mathbb{D}
and
H
preserve embeddings.
Then, given a heteromorphism
f\colon\alpha\to[\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern Proof 6.5
.

We show that the left oblong in the diagram below commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern \scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

Since
f
is a heteromorphism, we know that the outer square commutes. Further, the two squares on the right commute by naturality of
\kappa
and the assumption that
e_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
, is a coalgebra morphism. A simple diagram chasing implies that

is a heteromorphism.

Next we observe that
(S2)
is implied by a simple condition which can be stated entirely in terms of heteromorphisms, that is, typically, it is a condition in the base category of (pointed) relational structures.

.

Proof 6.6
.

Let

be be the corresponding homomorphism of (pointed)

-structures

, there exist coalgebra morphisms

such that

, for every

.

Remark 32
.

In fact,
(S2)
is equivalent to the version of
(S2’)
where we only care about morphisms
P\to H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern which arise from embeddings, via the correspondence in Proposition
27
.

Finally, by putting together the results from the last two sections, we are ready to state and prove an FVM theorem for the full logic. This time we state all assumptions for ease of reference.

Theorem 33
.

Let
H\colon\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}
be a functor and let
\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n}
and
\mathbb{D}
be comonads on categories
\mathcal{C}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
\mathcal{D}
, respectively, such that each of the arising categories of coalgebras are path categories and, furthermore,
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
has equalisers.

If
H
preserves embeddings and there exists a Kleisli law of type

\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}

satisfying
(S2’)
, then

A_{1}\equiv_{\mathbb{C}_{1}}B_{1},\;\ldots,\;A_{n}\equiv_{\mathbb{C}_{n}}B_{n}

implies

H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})\;\equiv_{\mathbb{D}}\;H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n}).

Proof 6.7
.

Given a collection of open pathwise-embeddings

\displaystyle F^{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(A_{1})\xleftarrow{f_{1}}Z_{1}\xrightarrow{g_{1}}F^{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(B_{1}),\quad\dots\ ,\ \ F^{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(A_{n})\xleftarrow{f_{n}}Z_{n}\xrightarrow{g_{n}}F^{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(B_{n})

in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{1}),\,\dots,\,\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{n})
, respectively, we wish to construct a pair of open pathwise-embeddings

\displaystyle F^{\mathbb{D}}(H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n}))\leftarrow Z\rightarrow F^{\mathbb{D}}(H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n}))

(15)

in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
.

Since there is a Kleisli law
\kappa\colon\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
, we know by Theorem
16
that
H
lifts to
\widehat{H}\colon\prod_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
.
Also, since
H
preserves embeddings, we know that
(S1)
holds by Proposition
29
.
Furthermore,
(S2)
holds too by Proposition
31
because we assumed that
\kappa
satisfies
(S2’)
.
Consequently, by Theorem
24
,
\widehat{H}
sends tuples of open pathwise-embeddings to open pathwise-embeddings.

We obtain a pair of open pathwise-embeddings

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle F^{\mathbb{C}_{i}}(A_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}{\vbox{\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern as the morphisms of coalgebras

This gives us that
\widehat{H}(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})
and
\widehat{H}(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})
are of the required type, as in (
15
).

Remark 34
.

Recall that the requirement that
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
has equalisers in Theorem
33
holds automatically in our examples, by Corollary
15
.
Furthermore, when we say that a Kleisli law
\kappa\colon\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
satisfies
(S2’)
, although
\kappa
is never mentioned in the phrasing of the axiom
(S2’)
, it appears implicitly in the definition of heteromorphisms.

{exa}

We can apply Theorem
33
to show that
\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
is preserved by taking disjoint unions of structures.
As the functor
\uplus
preserves embeddings, we only have to check
(S2’)
for the Kleisli law
\kappa_{A_{1},A_{2}}\colon{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{1}\uplus A_{2})\to{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{1})\uplus{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{2})
defined in Example
3.1
.
For a path
(P,\pi)
in
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{P}_{k}})
, consider a morphism
f\colon P\rightarrow A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
, where
(A_{1},\alpha_{1})
and
(A_{2},\alpha_{2})
are
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
-coalgebras, and
f
makes the following diagram of
(S2’)
commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1}\uplus A_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(\alpha_{1}\uplus\alpha_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{1})\uplus{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(A_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}\uplus\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

Recall from Remark
10
that the coalgebra map
\pi
defines a forest order
\sqsubseteq_{\pi}
on the universe of
P
, and similarly for coalgebra maps
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
. Furthermore, since
(P,\pi)
is a path,
(P,\sqsubseteq_{\pi})
is a finite linear order
x_{1}\sqsubseteq_{\pi}\dots\sqsubseteq_{\pi}x_{n}
.

We may assume without loss of generality that
f(x_{n})
is in
A_{1}
. Then,
(\alpha_{1}\uplus\alpha_{2})(f(x_{i}))
gives a word in
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}(\alpha_{1})
, which forms a finite linear order in the
\sqsubseteq_{\alpha_{i}}
order. By commutativity of the above diagram, this must be the same word as the down-to-right composition
\kappa({\mathbb{P}_{k}}(f)(\pi(x_{n})))
. But, by definition, the latter is just the reduction of the word on elements
f(x_{1}),\dots,f(x_{n})
to the positions where
f(x_{i})\in A_{1}
.

The same reasoning applies for the largest
j
such that
f(x_{j})
is in
A_{2}
.
Each of the two words obtained this way yields a path embedding
e_{i}\colon(P_{i},\pi_{i})\rightarrowtail(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
as the embedding of the induced substructure of
A_{i}
on given word letters.
Define a morphism
e_{0}\colon P\rightarrow P_{1}\uplus P_{2}
by sending an element to its image under
f
. By construction,
(e_{1}\uplus e_{2})\circ e_{0}
forms a decomposition of
f
and minimality of this decomposition is immediate, as any other decomposition of
f
would contain
P_{i}
as a subpath. We obtain the following FVM theorem for the
k
variable fragment:

\displaystyle A_{1}\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{1}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptA_{2}\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{2}

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad A_{1}\uplus A_{2}\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B_{1}\uplus B_{2}

The same reasoning goes through for
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
and
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
with coproducts over any index set
I
and for
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
with
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle R}}{{\vee}}
from Example
3.1
.

7.
FVM theorems for existential fragments

In
[
ALR
]
it is observed that we can also express preservation of existential formulas naturally in the setting of game comonads. Recall that
existential formulas
are first-order formulas without universal quantifiers and with negation only in front of atoms. Define

•

A\Rrightarrow_{\exists\mathbb{C}}B
if there exists a pathwise-embedding
F^{\mathbb{C}}(A)\to F^{\mathbb{C}}(B)
.

Then, it follows that
A\Rrightarrow_{\exists{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}B
iff, for every existential sentence
\varphi
of quantifier depth at most
k
,
A\models\varphi
implies
B\models\varphi
.
A similar statement holds for
A\Rrightarrow_{\exists{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}B
and the
k
-variable restriction of the existential fragment
[
AR23
]
.

It turns out that, by Lemma
22
, under the same conditions as in Theorem
33
we have a preservation theorem for
\Rrightarrow_{\exists\mathbb{C}}
. In fact, a weaker version of axiom
(S2’)
stated in terms of heteromorphisms suffices. This weaker axiom no longer requires the decomposition to be minimal, that is, we define:

(S2”)

For any path
(P,\pi)
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
and coalgebras
(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})
for
1\leq i\leq n
,
every heteromorphism
\pi\to[\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern has
some
decomposition through

H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}),

for some path embeddings
e_{i}\colon(P_{i},\pi_{i})\rightarrowtail(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
.

The corresponding FVM theorem for the existential fragments reads as follows.

Theorem 35
.

Let
H\colon\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\mathcal{D}
,
\mathbb{C}_{1},\dots,\mathbb{C}_{n}
and
\mathbb{D}
be exactly as in Theorem
33
, except that instead of
(S2’)
we only require
(S2”)
for the Kleisli law
\mathbb{D}\circ H\Rightarrow H\circ\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathbb{C}_{i}
. Then

A_{1}\Rrightarrow_{\exists\mathbb{C}_{1}}B_{1},\;\ldots,\;A_{n}\Rrightarrow_{\exists\mathbb{C}_{n}}B_{n}

implies

H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})\;\Rrightarrow_{\exists\mathbb{D}}\;H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n}).

Proof 7.1
.

Observe that in the proof of Lemma
22
the minimality of the decomposition was not needed. Further observe that the variant of
(S2)
where minimality is not required is obtained from
(S2”)
by the same proof as for Proposition
31
.
Consequently, we see that the lifting
\widehat{H}\colon\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C}_{i})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
of
H
preserves pathwise-embeddings.

Then, from
A_{1}\Rrightarrow_{\exists\mathbb{C}_{1}}B_{1},\;\ldots,\;A_{n}\Rrightarrow_{\exists\mathbb{C}_{n}}B_{n}
, witnessed by pathwise-embeddings

f_{1}\colon F^{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(A_{1})\to F^{\mathbb{C}_{2}}(B_{2}),\;\ldots,\;f_{n}\colon F^{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(A_{n})\to F^{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(B_{n})

we obtain a pathwise-embedding

\widehat{H}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{n})\colon\widehat{H}(F^{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(A_{1}),\ldots,F^{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(A_{n}))\to\widehat{H}(F^{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(B_{1}),\ldots,F^{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(B_{n})).

Finally, since
\widehat{H}
commutes with the cofree functors, i.e.
\widehat{H}(F^{\mathbb{C}_{1}}(A_{1}),\ldots,F^{\mathbb{C}_{n}}(A_{n}))=F^{\mathbb{D}}(H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n}))
, we have the required relation
H(A_{1},\dots,A_{n})\Rrightarrow_{\exists\mathbb{D}}H(B_{1},\dots,B_{n})
.

Remark 36
.

The recent papers of Abramsky, Laure and Reggio
[
AR24
,
ALR25
]
detail the categorical description of the relation
\Rrightarrow_{{}^{+}\mathbb{C}}
corresponding to the
positive fragments
, i.e. fragments without negation.
For two structures
A,B
, we write
A\Rrightarrow_{{}^{+}\mathbb{C}}B
if there exists a
positive bisimulation
from
F^{\mathbb{C}}(A)
to
F^{\mathbb{C}}(B)
, that is, there is a diagram of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathbb{C}}(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\mathbb{C}}(B)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where
f,g
are open pathwise-embeddings and
h
is a
bijection
.
Importantly, the definition of bijections requires
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
to be arboreal.

Consequently, in order to describe FVM theorems for the
\Rrightarrow_{{}^{+}\mathbb{C}}
relation, we need to describe conditions under which functors between arboreal categories of coalgebras preserve bijections.
We leave this as an open problem for future investigation.

8.
Abstract FVM theorems for products

In this section we show that FVM theorems for the operation of the categorical product of two structures are automatic, regardless of the chosen comonad. This shows the power of the categorical approach since the theorem applies to any situation where logical equivalence admits a comonadic characterisation.

Recall that the (categorical) product
\prod_{i}A_{i}

in the category
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
,
indexed by a set
I
, is the
\sigma
-structure with the universe being the product of the underlying universes
\prod_{i}A_{i}
. Further, for a relation
R
in
\sigma
, define its realisation in the product by

R^{\prod_{i}A_{i}}(\overline{a}_{1},\dots,\overline{a}_{n})\iff\ (\forall i)\ \ R^{A_{i}}(a_{1,i},\,\dots,\,a_{n,i})

This operation obeys the usual universal property of categorical products.
Namely, for any object
C
and morphisms
h_{i}\colon C\to A_{i}
for each
i\in I
, there is a
unique
morphism
\overline{h}\colon C\to\prod_{i}A_{i}
such that,
for every
i\in I
,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{h}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}

where
\pi_{i}\colon\prod_{i}A_{i}\to A_{i}
is the
i
th projection. In case of products of relational structures,
\overline{h}
sends
c
to the tuple
(h_{i}(c))_{i\in I}
.
Products in
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
work similarly, with the distinguished element of
\prod_{i}(A_{i},a_{i})
being the tuple
(a_{i})_{i}
.

8.1.
The positive existential and counting fragments

We fix an arbitrary comonad
\mathbb{C}
on a category
\mathcal{C}
with products.
Observe that, for a family of objects
\{A_{i}\}_{i}
of
\mathcal{C}
, indexed by a set
I
, we have morphisms

\mathbb{C}(\pi_{i})\colon\mathbb{C}(\prod\nolimits_{i}A_{i})\to\mathbb{C}(A_{i})

for each
i\in I
and, by the universal property of products, also the morphism

\kappa_{\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu}\colon\mathbb{C}(\prod\nolimits_{i}A_{i})\to\prod\nolimits_{i}\mathbb{C}(A_{i}).

Applying Theorem
3
immediately yields the following FVM theorem for positive existential fragments.

Theorem 37
.

Given collections of
\sigma
-structures
\{A_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\{B_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, indexed by a common set
I
, and a comonad
\mathbb{C}
on a category
\mathcal{C}
with products. Then,

\textstyle\left(\forall i\in I.\;A_{i}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}_{i}}B_{i}\right)\quad\mbox{implies}\quad\prod_{i}A_{i}\Rrightarrow_{\exists^{+}\mathbb{C}_{i}}\prod_{i}B_{i}.

A direct calculation reveals that
\kappa
is natural in the choice of
\{A_{i}\}_{i}
and, furthermore, is a Kleisli law.

Lemma 38
.

\kappa
is a Kleisli law.

Proof 8.1
.

First, observe that by the definition of products and how
\kappa
is defined, we have that

\mathbb{C}(\pi_{i})=\pi_{i}\circ\kappa

(16)

for every
i
. From this we show commutativity of the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}\left(\prod_{i}A_{i}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}(A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}

By universality of the products, this implies the counit axiom of Kleisli laws that is
(K1)
for the operation
H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu)=\prod_{i}A_{i}
. To show commutativity of the pentagon above, compute:

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\circ\left(\prod\nolimits_{i}\varepsilon\right)\circ\kappa

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\circ\pi_{i}\circ\kappa

products

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\circ\mathbb{C}(\pi_{i})

(
16
)

\displaystyle=\pi_{i}\circ\varepsilon

naturality

Next, to show
(K2)
for the operation
H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu)=\prod_{i}A_{i}
, we employ the same strategy. We wish to show commutativity of the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(\prod_{i}A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}(A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{2}(\prod_{i}A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}(A_{i}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}^{2}(A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}^{2}(A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}^{2}(A_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(\kappa)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}

To do so, we compute:

\displaystyle\pi_{i}\circ\left(\prod\nolimits_{i}\delta\right)\circ\kappa

\displaystyle=\delta\circ\pi_{i}\circ\kappa

products

\displaystyle=\delta\circ\mathbb{C}(\pi_{i})

(
16
)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}^{2}(\pi_{i})\circ\delta

naturality

\displaystyle=\mathbb{C}(\pi_{i})\circ\mathbb{C}(\kappa)\circ\delta

(
16
)

\displaystyle=\pi_{i}\circ\kappa\circ\mathbb{C}(\kappa)\circ\delta

(
16
)

As a corollary of Lemma
38
and Theorem
9
, we obtain an abstract FVM for counting fragments.

Theorem 39
.

Given the same assumptions as in Theorem
37
,

\textstyle\left(\forall i\in I.\;A_{i}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}B_{i}\right)\quad\mbox{implies}\quad\prod_{i}A_{i}\equiv_{\#\mathbb{C}}\prod_{i}B_{i}.

8.2.
The full fragments

We also have an FVM theorem for products and the equivalence
\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}
.
We prove this by checking the assumptions of Theorem
33
.
We first check that products preserve embeddings.

Lemma 40
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a category with products and a weak factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
. Then, for any collection of embeddings
\{m_{i}\colon C_{i}\rightarrowtail D_{i}\}_{i}
the product
\prod_{i}m_{i}\colon\prod_{i}C_{i}\to\prod_{i}D_{i}
is an embedding too.

Proof 8.2
.

From the definition of weak factorisation systems it is enough to show that
\prod_{i}m_{i}
is weakly orthogonal to any quotient
e\in\mathcal{E}
, i.e.
e\pitchfork\prod_{i}m_{i}
. To this end, assume we have the following a commutative square.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}D_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}m_{i}}

Upon fixing
i
, we can compose this square with the naturality square for product projections.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}D_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}

Then, since
e\pitchfork m_{i}
, there is some
d_{i}\colon B\to C_{i}
such that
d_{i}\circ e=\pi_{i}\circ f
and
m_{i}\circ d=\pi_{i}\circ g
.

Set
\langle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle d_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle d_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern to be the unique morphism

obtained from the cone

which gives

and, similarly,

0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern which gives the second required equation, i.e.
\prod_{i}m_{i}\circ\langle\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle d_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle d_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu\rangle=g
.

Recall that in order to specify
\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}
, we need
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
to be a path category, that is, it comes equipped with a proper factorisation system
(\overline{\mathcal{E}},\overline{\mathcal{M}})
and a choice of path objects
\mathcal{P}\subseteq\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
.
As before, we consider the setting where
(\overline{\mathcal{E}},\overline{\mathcal{M}})
is obtained as the lifting of a proper factorisation system
(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{M})
on the base category
\mathcal{C}
, as in Lemma
13
.
In this case, a morphism of coalgebras
(A,\alpha)\to(B,\beta)
is a quotient (i.e. is in
\overline{\mathcal{E}}
) if the underlying morphism
A\to B
is a quotient (i.e. is in
\mathcal{E}
).

The crucial requirement of our abstract FVM theorems for products is that
paths are closed under quotients
, that is, for any quotient

X\twoheadrightarrow Y

if
X
is a path then so is
Y
. The following lemma shows that this is a sufficient requirement for our path category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
to ensure the second assumption of Theorem
33
.

Lemma 41
.

If
\mathbb{C}
preserves embeddings and paths are closed under quotients in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
,
then
H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu)=\prod_{i}A_{i}
satisfies axiom
(S2’)
.

Proof 8.3
.

We assume that
(Q,\rho)
is a path in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
and
(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
are arbitrary
\mathbb{C}
-coalgebras. Further, assume that we are given a morphism
f\colon P\to\prod_{i}A_{i}
which makes the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(\prod_{i}A_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\mathbb{C}(A_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}\alpha_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\kappa}

It is an easy observation that the composition
\pi_{i}\circ f
with the
i
th projection
\pi_{i}\colon\prod_{i}A_{i}\to A_{i}
is a coalgebra morphism
(Q,\rho)\to(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
. Since
\mathbb{C}
preserves embeddings, Lemma
13
applies and we can take the factorisation of this morphism in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q,\rho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q_{i},\rho_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(A_{i},\alpha_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}}

where
q_{i}
is a quotient and
e_{i}
is an embedding. Since
(Q,\rho)
is a path and
q_{i}
is a quotient, we see that
(Q_{i},\rho_{i})
is a path by our assumptions. Consequently, we have a decomposition of
f
into

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}Q_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}e_{i}}

(17)

where
q
is obtained from the universal property of products, from the collection of morphisms
\{q_{i}\}_{i}
.

To show the minimality of this decomposition, let
f
also decompose as

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}S_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}A_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i}m_{i}}

(18)

where
m_{i}
are path embeddings
(S_{i},\omega_{i})\rightarrowtail(A_{i},\alpha_{i})
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
. By post-composing both homomorphisms in (
17
) and (
18
) with the projection
\pi_{i}\colon\prod_{i}A_{i}\to A_{i}
we obtain a commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{i}\circ r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}

Now, observe that
q_{i}
is a quotient and
m_{i}
is an embedding and so there is a diagonal morphism
d_{i}\colon Q_{i}\to S_{i}
such that
e_{i}=m_{i}\circ d_{i}
. It remains to check that
d_{i}
is a morphism of coalgebras
(Q_{i},\rho_{i})\to(S_{i},\omega_{i})
. This is a consequence of the fact that
\mathbb{C}(m_{i})
is an embedding and that the right square and the surrounding rectangle of the following diagram commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(Q_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(S_{i})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(A_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(d_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{C}(m_{i})}

Indeed, let
x\in Q_{i}
. By chasing the above diagram we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{C}(m_{i})\circ\omega_{i}\circ d_{i}=\alpha_{i}\circ m_{i}\circ d_{i}=\mathbb{C}(m_{i})\circ\mathbb{C}(d_{i})\circ\rho_{i}

and since
\mathbb{C}(m_{i})
is a monomorphism
\omega_{i}\circ d_{i}=\mathbb{C}(d_{i})\circ\rho_{i}
, as required.

To summarise, we have shown that any category
\mathcal{C}
with products admits a Kleisli law for any comonad
\mathbb{C}
on
\mathcal{C}
(Lemma
38
). If, furthermore,
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
has equalisers then
\prod_{i}
automatically satisfies assumptions of Theorem
33
by Lemmas
40
and
41
.

As a corollary we obtain a general FVM theorem for products, with assumptions that hold true for all the game comonads studied so far in the literature (cf. Example
8.2
below).

Theorem 42
.

Let
\mathbb{C}
be a comonad on a category
\mathcal{C}
with products, such that
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
is a path category. If
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
has equalisers and paths are closed under quotients then

\textstyle\left(\forall i\in I.\;A_{i}\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}B_{i}\right)\quad\mbox{implies}\quad\prod_{i}A_{i}\,\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}\,\prod_{i}B_{i}.

As a consequence of Corollary
15
we obtain the following specialised version of the above theorem.

Corollary 43
.

If a comonad
\mathbb{C}
on
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
or
\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
preserves embeddings and if
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
is a path category with paths closed under quotients, then

\textstyle\left(\forall i\in I.\;A_{i}\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}B_{i}\right)\quad\mbox{implies}\quad\prod_{i}A_{i}\,\equiv_{\mathbb{C}}\,\prod_{i}B_{i}.

{exa}

In the case of
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
,
it is immediate that
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
preserves embeddings. Furthermore, for a surjective coalgebra morphism
(P,\pi)\to(A,\alpha)
in
\mathsf{EM}({\mathbb{E}_{k}})
such that
(P,\pi)
is a path, we also have that
(A,\alpha)
is a path since, in view of Remark
10
, we map a finite linear order onto a forest.

The same is true for our example comonads
{\mathbb{P}_{k}}
,
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
, yielding product FVM theorems for the bounded quantifier rank, bounded variable and bounded modal depth fragments and their positive existential and counting variants.

{exa}

More generally, the assumptions of Theorem
43
hold for any comonad
\mathbb{C}
which preserves embeddings and such that the category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
is an
arboreal category
, in sense of
[
AR21
]
, by Lemma 3.5 therein.

In particular, all FVM theorems for products from this section hold for
hybrid logic
because of the hybrid comonad of
[
AM22
]
,
guarded logics
captured by guarded comonads in
[
AM21
]
, the
bounded conjunction
and
bounded quantifier rank
fragments of the
k
-variable first-order logic captured in
[
MS22
]
and
[
Pai20
]
, respectively.

Remark 44
.

Since monadic second-order logic (MSO) does not have a FVM theorem for products (see e.g.
[
Mak04
]
), we can conclude that there does not exist a comonad for MSO over
\mathcal{R}(\sigma)
which satisfies the conditions of Corollary
43
.
This limitation can be overcome by encoding the standard semantics of MSO using the technique of enrichment, described in Section
9
, applied to a modified
{\mathbb{E}_{k}}
comonad on two-sorted structures.
The full details of this construction appear in the follow up paper
[
JMS22a
]
.

9.
Adding equality and other enrichments

Recall that the relations
\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}
and
\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}
express logical equivalence with respect to the logic
without
equality.
The standard way to add equality to the language is to extend the signature
\sigma
with an additional binary relation symbol
I(\cdot,\cdot)
, and provide a
translation

\mathfrak{t}^{I}\colon\mathcal{R}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}(\sigma\cup\{I\})

which interprets this new relational symbol as equality, i.e.
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)
is the
\sigma\cup\{I\}
-structure extension of
A
by setting
I(a,b)
if and only if
a=b
. Since our game comonads are defined uniformly for any signature, they are also defined over
\mathcal{R}(\sigma\cup\{I\})
, giving us:

•

\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B)
iff
A
and
B
are logically equivalent w.r.t. the fragment of first-order logic consisting of formulas of quantifier depth
{\leq}\,k
with equality, and

•

\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)\equiv_{{\mathbb{P}_{k}}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B)
iff
A
and
B
are logically equivalent w.r.t. the fragment of first-order logic consisting of formulas in
k
-variables with equality.

Moreover, an operation which commutes with the translation
\mathfrak{t}^{I}
lifts FVM theorems for fragments without equality to fragments with equality. For example, disjoint unions satisfy that
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A\uplus B)=\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A)\uplus\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B)
and, therefore, if
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{1})\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{1})
, and
\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{2})\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{2})
, then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{1}\uplus A_{2})

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt=\kern 5.0pt\mkern 8.0mu\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{1})\uplus\mathfrak{t}^{I}(A_{2})

\displaystyle\kern 5.0pt\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{1})\uplus\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{2})=\mathfrak{t}^{I}(B_{1}\uplus B_{2})

which shows that
A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
and
B_{1}\uplus B_{2}
are logically equivalent w.r.t. formulas of quantifier depth
{\leq}\,k
with equality.

In general, similar translations can be used for other logic extensions.
For example, in
[
BU22
]
a translation is used to obtain description logics using
{\mathbb{M}_{k}}
.
Nevertheless, the above technique applies verbatim to varying translations, operations and logics.
We arrive at the following simple but important fact, that greatly increases the applicability of the results in the previous sections.

Theorem 45
.

For
1\leq i\leq n+1
, let
\mathcal{L}_{i}
and
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i}
be logics and
\mathfrak{t}_{i}
a translation such that
{A\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}B}
iff
{\mathfrak{t}_{i}(A)\equiv_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i}}\mathfrak{t}_{i}(B)}
. Further, let
H^{\prime}
be an
n
-ary operation satisfying the
FVM theorem from
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{n}
to
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{n+1}
, with respect to translated structures. That is:

\mathfrak{t}_{1}(A_{1})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{1}}\mathfrak{t}_{1}(B_{1}),\;\ldots,\;\mathfrak{t}_{n}(A_{n})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{n}}\mathfrak{t}_{n}(B_{n})

implies

commutes with the translations in the sense that

. That is:

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu)

Proof 9.1
.

Assume that
A_{i}\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{i}}B_{i}
, for every
i=1,\dots,n
.
By our assumptions, we obtain that
\mathfrak{t}_{i}(A_{i})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{i}}\mathfrak{t}_{i}(B_{i})
for every
i=1,\dots,n
and, therefore,

H^{\prime}(\mathfrak{t}_{1}(A_{1}),\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{n}(A_{n}))\equiv_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{n+1}}H^{\prime}(\mathfrak{t}_{1}(B_{1}),\dots,\mathfrak{t}_{n}(B_{n}))

Then, by a straightforward calculation

\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{n+1}(H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu))\cong H^{\prime}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\mathfrak{t}_{i}(A_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\mathfrak{t}_{i}(A_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\mathfrak{t}_{i}(A_{i})\hfil$\crcr}}})\equiv_{\mathcal{L}^{\prime}_{n+1}}H^{\prime}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern
