0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu))

which, by our assumptions, implies that

H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle A_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu)\equiv_{\mathcal{L}_{n+1}}H(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle B_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}\mkern 1.0mu).

{exa}

Let
\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}\colon\mathcal{R}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}(\sigma^{\texttt{com}})
be a translation of
\sigma
-structures into the extension
\sigma^{\texttt{com}}
of
\sigma\cup\{I\}
with an extra binary relation
\texttt{com}(\cdot,\cdot)
. The interpretation of
com
in
\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}(A)
consists of all pairs
(a,b)
appearing in the same component of the Gaifman graph of
A
, that is, pairs
(a,b)
such that there is a path
a\leftrightsquigarrow x_{1}\leftrightsquigarrow\dots\leftrightsquigarrow x_{n}\leftrightsquigarrow b
where
x\leftrightsquigarrow y
holds whenever
x
and
y
are in a common tuple in an
R^{A}
of some
R\in\sigma
.

It is easy to see that
\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}(A)\equiv_{{\mathbb{E}_{k}}}\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}(B)
expresses logical equivalence in the bounded quantifier depth fragment extended with the connectivity predicate, akin to
[
Boj21
,
SSV22
]
. Since
\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}(A\uplus B)\cong\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}(A)\uplus\mathfrak{t}^{\texttt{com}}(B)
, we obtain, by Theorem
45
and Example
6.2
, an FVM theorem for disjoint unions and the quantifier rank
k
fragment of said logic.
While still being polynomial-time computable, the connectivity relation is not definable in bounded quantifier depth fragments of first-order logic,
and so this is a proper extension.

{exa}

Continuing from Example
8.2
, consider
\mathfrak{t}\colon\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma^{G})
where
\sigma^{G}
is the extension of the modal signature
\sigma
with the
global relation

G(a,b)
, true in
\mathfrak{t}(A,a)
for any pair of elements. Then, it is immediate that
\mathfrak{t}((A,a)\times(B,b))\cong\mathfrak{t}(A,a)\times\mathfrak{t}(B,b)
, giving us by Theorem
45
an FVM theorem for products and modal logic with global modalities.

{exa}

As an example of Theorem
45
where the two operations differ, we consider the notion of
weak (bi)simulation
where transitions along a distinguished relation
S\in\sigma
are considered to occur silently.
We can model weak bisimulation by considering bisimulation between translated structures where
\mathfrak{t}\colon\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)\to\mathcal{R}_{*}(\sigma)
replaces all relations
R
with their closure under sequences of prior and subsequent
S
transitions.

For pointed structures
A_{1}=(A_{1},a_{1})
and
A_{2}=(A_{2},a_{2})
, there is an operation
A_{1}\vee A_{2}
which adds a new initial point
\star
to
A_{1}\uplus A_{2}
. We extend the transitions with
R(\star,(i,x))
, for
R\in\sigma
, whenever
R^{A_{i}}(a_{i},x)
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
and
x\in A_{i}
.
This construction satisfies

\mathfrak{t}(A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\vee}}A_{2})\cong\mathfrak{t}(A_{1})\vee\mathfrak{t}(A_{2})

by design, and there is a Kleisli law

\kappa_{A_{1},A_{2}}\colon{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A_{1}\vee A_{2})\rightarrow{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A_{1})\vee{\mathbb{M}_{k}}(A_{2}),

via a similar construction to that used in Example
3.1
. This yields an FVM theorem:

\displaystyle A_{1}\Rrightarrow_{W_{k}}B_{1}\kern 5.0pt\mbox{and}\kern 5.0ptA_{2}\Rrightarrow_{W_{k}}B_{2}

\displaystyle\mbox{implies}\quad A_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\vee}}A_{2}\Rrightarrow_{W_{k}}B_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle S}}{{\vee}}B_{2}

where
A\Rrightarrow_{W_{k}}B
indicates that
A
weakly simulates
B
up to depth
k
.

10.
Conclusion

We presented a categorical approach to the composition methods, and specifically Feferman–Vaught–Mostowski theorems.
We exploit game comonads to encapsulate the logics and their model comparison games.
Surprising connections to classical constructions in category theory, and especially the monad theory of bilinear maps emerged from this approach, cf. Section
5.1
.

For finite model theorists, our work provides a novel high-level account of many FVM theorems, abstracting away from individual logics and constructions.
Furthermore, concrete instances of these theorems are verified purely semantically, by finding a suitable collection homomorphisms forming a Kleisli law satisfying
(S2’)
, instead of the usual delicate verification that strategies of model comparison games compose.
For game comonads, we provide a much needed tool that enables us to handle logical relationships between structures as they are transformed or viewed in terms of different logics.

The FVM results in this paper, combined with judicious use of the techniques described in Section
9
can be pushed significantly further.
FVM theorems and the compositional method are of particular significance in the setting of monadic second-order (MSO) logic
[
Gur85
]
. Exploiting the results we have presented for the composition methods, the follow up paper
[
JMS22a
]
gives a comonadic semantics for MSO, and develops a semantic account of Courcelle’s algorithmic meta-theorems
[
CE12
]
.

The original theorem of Feferman–Vaught
[
FV59
]
is very flexible, including incorporating structure on the indices of families of models to be combined by an operation, as well as the models themselves. This aspect is currently outside the scope of our comonadic methods.
An ad-hoc adaptation of our approach also gives a proof of the FVM theorem for free amalgamations, but this does not follow directly from our theorems. Developing these extensions is left to future work.

The present work bears some resemblance to Turi and Plotkin’s bialgebraic semantics
[
TP97
]
, a categorical model of structural operational semantics (SOS)
[
Plo81
]
.
Turi and Plotkin noticed that the SOS rules assigning behaviour to syntax could be abstracted as a certain distributive law
\lambda
.
An algebra encodes the composition operations of the syntax, and a coalgebra the behaviour.
If this pair is suitably compatible with
\lambda
, forming a so-called
\lambda
-bialgebra, then crucially bisimulation is a congruence with respect to the composition operations.
Both bialgebraic semantics and our work presented in this paper give
a categorical account of well-behaved composition operations, with the interaction between composition and observable behaviour mediated by some form of distributive law. There are also essential differences: bialgebraic semantics typically focuses on assigning behaviour to syntax,
whereas FVM theorems encompass operations on models.
Our FVM theorems are parametric in a choice of logic or observable behaviour, while the notion of bisimulation in bialgebraic semantics is fixed by the coalgebra signature functor.
The natural presence of positive existential and counting quantifier variants of FVM theorems, and the incorporation of resource parameters, do not seem to have a direct analogue in bialgebraic semantics. The similarities are intriguing, and exploring the relationship between bialgebraic semantics and our approach to FVM theorems is left to future work.

Appendix A
Parametric relative right adjoints

In this section we show how the axioms
(S1)
and
(S2)
from Section
5.2
relate to mathematical concepts that have previously appeared in the literature, specifically parametric relative adjoints.
Parametric adjoints, also sometimes called local adjoints, originate in the work of Street
[
Str00
]
.
We use their equivalent definition due to Weber
[
Web04
,
Web07
,
BMW12
]
.
Relative adjoints are a common tool in the semantics of programming languages, see for example
[
ACU15
]
, and are connected to relative comonads which can be used to encapsulate the translations discussed in Section
9

[
AS21
]
. We will be interested in a combination of these two notions.

We first review the required terminology. By
\mathcal{T}\mathbin{\downarrow}A
we denote the

slice category

consisting of pairs
(X,f)
, where
f\colon X\to A
is a morphism in
\mathcal{T}
. A morphism
(X,f)\to(Y,g)
between objects in
\mathcal{T}\mathbin{\downarrow}A
is a morphism
h\colon X\to Y
such that
f=g\circ h
. A functor
{F\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{S}}
is said to be a
parametric right adjoint
if, for each object
A
of
\mathcal{C}
, the functor

F_{A}:\mathcal{T}\mathbin{\downarrow}A\to\mathcal{S}\mathbin{\downarrow}F(A)

sending
f\colon X\to A
to
F(f)\colon F(X)\to F(A)
, is a right adjoint.
Finally, a functor
R\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{U}
is a
relative right adjoint
between a category
\mathcal{T}
and a functor
I\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{U}
if there is a functor
L\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}
and a natural bijection between sets of morphisms

L(S)\to T
in
\mathcal{T}

I(S)\to R(T)
in
\mathcal{U}

These definitions motivate our definition of parametric relative adjoints for categories of coalgebras with a selected class of path objects and a class of embeddings.
For simplicity we consider unary
\widehat{H}:\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
.
We write
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}
for the class of paths in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
and
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
respectively.

For
D\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})
, let
I_{D}
be the inclusion functor

I_{D}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}D\to\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}D

where
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}D
and
{\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}D}
denote the full subcategories of the comma category
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\downarrow}D
consisting of pairs
(X,e)
where
e
is an embedding or a path embedding, respectively.

\widehat{H}
is a
parametric relative right adjoint
if, for every
C\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
, the functor

\widehat{H}_{C}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}C\kern 5.0pt\longrightarrow\kern 5.0pt\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C),

which sends a path embedding
e
, with
e\colon X\rightarrowtail C
, to the embedding
\widehat{H}(e)
of type
\widehat{H}(X)\rightarrowtail\widehat{H}(C)
, is a relative right adjoint between
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}C
and
I_{\widehat{H}(C)}
, which we will denote simply by
I
. Following the above terminology, the last condition assumes the existence of a functor
L\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}C

such that there is a natural bijection between morphisms:

L(e)\to f
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}C

I(e)\to\widehat{H}_{C}(f)
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)

By unwrapping the definitions, we see that the natural bijection sends an embedding
m\colon P^{\prime}\to Q
which makes the diagram on the left below commute to an embedding
m^{\dagger}\colon P\to\widehat{H}(Q)
which makes the diagram on the right below commute.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(e)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(Q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(f)}

Furthermore, the assignment
m\mapsto m^{\dagger}
has an inverse
t\mapsto t_{\dagger}
and the naturality can be rephrased by the following two rules:

•

For any
g\colon Q\rightarrowtail Q^{\prime}
and
f^{\prime}\colon Q^{\prime}\rightarrowtail C
such that
f=f^{\prime}\circ g
:
(g\circ m)^{\dagger}=\widehat{H}(g)\circ m^{\dagger}
.

•

For any
h\colon S\rightarrowtail P
and
e^{\prime}\colon S\to\widehat{H}(C)
such that
e^{\prime}=e\circ h
:
(t\circ h)_{\dagger}=t_{\dagger}\circ L(h)
.

In Proposition
A.2
below we show that under the assumption that
\widehat{H}
satisfies
(S1)
, the axiom
(S2)
is equivalent to
\widehat{H}
being a parametric relative right adjoint.
For the proof of the equivalence with parametric relative right adjoints we adapt a standard characterisation of left adjoints in terms of mapping on objects satisfying the expected universal property to the setting of relative adjoints. This is what we do next.

Recall that a functor
R\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{S}
has a left adjoint if and only if, for every object
b\in\mathcal{S}
, there is an object
L(b)
in
\mathcal{T}
and a morphism
\eta_{b}\colon b\to R(L(b))
such that, for any other
f\colon b\to R(a)
there is a unique
f^{*}\colon L(b)\to a
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(L(b))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(a)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(f^{*})}

A similar statement holds for relative adjoints too, with a proof that follows essentially the same steps.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
R\colon\mathcal{T}\to\mathcal{U}
and
I\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{U}
be functors and further assume that there is

•

a mapping on objects
L\colon\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{S})\to\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{T})
and

•

a morphism
\eta_{a}\colon I(a)\to RL(a)
, for every
a\in\mathcal{S}
, such that for any
f\colon I(a)\to R(b)
there is a unique
{\overline{f}\colon L(a)\to b}
such that the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RL(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L(a)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{a}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R(\,\overline{f}\,)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{f}}

Then, the mapping
L
extends to a functor
L\colon\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{T}
and there is a natural bijection

\rho:\hom(L(A),B)\xrightarrow{\kern 4.09723pt\cong\kern 4.09723pt}\hom(I(A),R(B)).

witnessing that
R
is a relative right adjoint.

With this we can now prove the promised statement.

Proposition A.2
.

Assuming
(S1)
,
\widehat{H}
satisfies
(S2)
if and only if
\widehat{H}
is a parametric relative right adjoint.

Proof A.1
.

(\Rightarrow)
Assuming
(S2)
and fixing
C\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
, we define the relative left adjoint
L_{C}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}C
of
\widehat{H}_{C}
. Following Lemma
A.1
, it is enough to define the appropriate mapping on objects

L_{C}\colon\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C))\to\mathrm{obj}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}C)

and a collection of morphisms
\eta_{e}\colon I(e)\to\widehat{H}_{C}L_{C}(e)
, for every object
e
of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)
i.e. an embedding
e\colon\pi\rightarrowtail\widehat{H}(C)
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
.

Given
e\colon\pi\rightarrowtail\widehat{H}(C)
, we denote by

\pi\xrightarrow{\kern 4.09723ptg_{e}\kern 4.09723pt}\widehat{H}(\pi_{e})\xrightarrow{\widehat{H}(h_{e})}\widehat{H}(C)

its minimal decomposition. Define
L_{C}(e)
as
h_{e}
and
\eta_{e}
as
g_{e}
. The fact that
g_{e}
with
\widehat{H}(h_{e})
is a decomposition of
e
expresses the fact that
\eta_{e}
is indeed a morphism
I(e)\to\widehat{H}_{C}(L_{C}(e))
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)
, that is, we have the following.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(\pi_{e})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(L_{C}(e))}

(19)

Next, we check the universal property of
\eta_{e}
. Any morphism
z\colon I(e)\to\widehat{H}_{C}(g)
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)
, by definition, corresponds to having a commutative triangle in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(\rho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(g)}

for a path embedding
g\colon\rho\rightarrowtail C
. Consequently, we have a commutative square:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(\pi_{e})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(\rho)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(C)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(h_{e})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}(g)}

By minimality of the decomposition (
19
), there is a morphism
z^{*}\colon\pi_{e}\to\rho
such that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{e}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

i.e.
z^{*}
is a morphism
\pi_{e}\to\rho
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}C
. Furthermore, by (S1),
\widehat{H}
preserves embeddings. Hence,
\widehat{H}(g)
is a monomorphism and the previous two diagrams give us that
z=\widehat{H}(z^{*})\circ g_{e}
. In other words, the following diagram commutes in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I(e)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}_{C}(L_{C}(e))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}_{C}(g)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{e}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{H}_{C}(z^{*})}

Unicity of
z^{*}
follows from the fact that
g
is a monomorphism.

(\Leftarrow)
Assume
L_{C}
is the relative left adjoint of
\widehat{H}_{C}
from
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}C
to the embedding functor
I\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{D}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)\hookrightarrow\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)
, for a fixed
C\in\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
, and let
\tau
be the corresponding natural bijection

\tau\colon\hom(L_{C}(e),f)\xrightarrow{\kern 4.09723pt\cong\kern 4.09723pt}\hom(I(e),\widehat{H}_{C}(f)).

Next, given a path embedding
e\colon\pi\rightarrowtail\widehat{H}(C)
, observe that it is an object of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)
. Set

e^{\prime}\colon\pi^{\prime}\rightarrowtail C\quad\mbox{and}\quad e_{0}\colon\pi\to\widehat{H}(\pi^{\prime})

to be
L_{C}(e)
and the underlying morphism of the unit
\eta_{e}\colon I(e)\to\widehat{H}_{C}L_{C}(e)
of the relative adjunction, respectively, i.e.
e_{0}=\tau(\mathrm{id}\colon L_{C}(e)\to L_{C}(e))
. Observe that, the fact that
\eta_{e}
is a morphism in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)
says precisely that
e
is equal to the composition

\pi\xrightarrow{\kern 4.09723pte_{0}\kern 4.09723pt}\widehat{H}(\pi^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\widehat{H}(L_{C}(e))}\widehat{H}(C).

For minimality of this decomposition, consider an alternative decomposition of
e

\pi\xrightarrow{\kern 4.09723ptg_{0}\kern 4.09723pt}\widehat{H}(\rho)\xrightarrow{\widehat{H}(g_{1})}\widehat{H}(C)

where
g_{1}
is a path embedding
g_{1}\colon\rho\rightarrowtail C
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
. The fact that it is a decomposition of
e
expresses the fact that
g_{0}
is a morphism
I(e)\to\widehat{H}_{C}(g_{1})
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{D})\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}\widehat{H}(C)
. Therefore, the morphism
\tau^{-1}(g_{0})
gives
L_{C}(e)\to g_{1}
in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathbb{C}}\mathbin{\rotatebox[origin={c}]{270.0}{$\rightarrowtail$}}C
. In other words, the underlying morphism of coalgebras of
\tau^{-1}(g_{0})
is of type
\pi^{\prime}\to\rho
and
e^{\prime}
is equal to
g_{1}\circ\tau^{-1}(g_{0})
in
\mathsf{EM}(\mathbb{C})
.

Remark A.3
.

For a functor
\widehat{H}
to be a parametric relative right adjoint means, by Lemma
A.1
specialised to
\widehat{H}_{C}
, that every path embedding
P\rightarrowtail\widehat{H}(C)
has a decomposition as
e_{0}\colon P\to\widehat{H}(P^{\prime})
followed by
\widehat{H}(e_{1})
for some
e_{1}\colon P^{\prime}\rightarrowtail A
which is minimal in the following stronger sense. For any other such decomposition
\widehat{H}(g_{1})\circ g_{0}
of
e
, not only there is a unique
h
such that
e_{1}=g_{1}\circ h
, as in
(S2)
, but moreover we have that
g_{0}=\widehat{H}(h)\circ e_{0}
.
Consequently, Proposition
A.2
shows that minimal decompositions given by
(S2)
enjoy this stronger property.
